
LESSON Integrating Functions by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186068507
Calculate $\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{36 -(3x - 2)^2}} \, \textrm{d}x.$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{36-(3x-2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{36(1-(3x-2)^{2}/36)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{36}\cdot \sqrt{1-(3x-2)^{2}/36}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{6\sqrt{1-(3x-2)^{2}/36}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{6}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(3x-2)^{2}/36}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{6}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((3x-2)/6)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-u^2}}\,\textrm{d}u = \arcsin u + C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $\left(\dfrac{3x-2}{6}\right).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=\dfrac{3x-2}{6}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x} = \dfrac{1}{2} \quad \Longrightarrow \quad \textrm{d}x = 2\,\textrm{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{36-(3x-2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{6}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((3x-2)/6)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{6}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{3x-2}{6})+C\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186068504
Calculate $\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{x^2-2x + 3}\, \textrm{d}x.$

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\eqalign{
x^2-2x + 3
& = x^2 - 2 \cdot x+ (1^2 - 1^2) + 3 \[3pt]
& = (x^2 - 2 \cdot x+ 1^2) - 1 + 3 \[3pt]
& = (x-1)^2+2
}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-2x+3}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x-1)^{2}+2}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-1)/\sqrt{2})^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{1+u^2} \, \textrm{d}u
=
\arctan u + C.
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $\left(\dfrac{x-1}{\sqrt{2}}\right).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\dfrac{x-1}{\sqrt{2}}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \sqrt{2}\,\textrm{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-2x+3}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-1)/\sqrt{2})^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\cdot \sqrt{2}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\operatorname{arctan}⁡(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{2}})+C\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186068505
Calculate ${\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{4x - 4x^2}} \, \textrm{d}x}.$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}4x-4{x}^{2} & =-(4{x}^{2}-4x) \\  & =-((2x)^{2}-2\cdot 2x+1-1) \\  & =-((2x-1)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(2x-1)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4x-4{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(2x-1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-u^2}}\,\textrm{d}u = \arcsin u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(2x - 1).$  

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u =2x - 1.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \dfrac12\textrm{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4x-4{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(2x-1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot \frac{1}{2}\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(2x-1)+C.\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186068506
Evaluate the integral $\displaystyle{\int_{1+2/\sqrt{3}}^{3} \dfrac{1}{|x-1|\sqrt{x^2-2x}}\, \textrm{d}x}.$

First, we complete the square for the expression inside the square root:

$$
\eqalign{
    x^2-2x&= x^2 -2x +(1-1)\[3pt]
              &= (x^2 -2x +1)-1\[3pt]
         &= (x-1)^2-1
         
  }
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\int_{1+2/\sqrt{3}}^{3} \dfrac{1}{|x-1|\sqrt{x^2-2x}}\, \textrm{d}x 
= \int_{1+2/\sqrt{3}}^{3} \dfrac{1}{|x-1|\sqrt{(x-1)^2-1}}\, \textrm{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2-1}} \, \textrm{d}u = \textrm{arcsec} \, u +C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $x-1.$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x-1.
$$

 
Differentiating, we get

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $1+\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}}$ | $3$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}}$ | $2$ |

Therefore,

$$
\eqalign{
    \int_{1+2/\sqrt{3}}^{3} \dfrac{1}{|x-1|\sqrt{x^2-2x}}\, \textrm{d}x 
&=  \int_{1+2/\sqrt{3}}^{3}\dfrac{1}{|x-1|\sqrt{(x-1)^2-1}}\,\textrm{d}x \[5pt]
&=\int_{2/\sqrt{3}}^{2} \dfrac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2-1}}\, \textrm{d}u\[5pt]
&=  \textrm{arcsec}\, {u}\Big|_{2/\sqrt{3}}^{2}\[5pt]
&= \textrm{arcsec} \left(2\right)  - \textrm{arcsec}\left(\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}} \right)\[5pt]
&=\left(\dfrac{\pi}{3}-\dfrac{\pi}{6}\right)\[5pt]
&=\dfrac{\pi}{6}.

}
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186070030 Introduction
Consider the following integral:

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{x^2+2x+5}\, \textrm{d}x
$$

This integral doesn't resemble any of the standard integrals we have encountered so far.

However, notice that we can complete the square in the denominator as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2x+5 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x+5 \\  & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x+({1}^{2}-{1}^{2})+5 \\  & =({x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2})-{1}^{2}+5 \\  & =(x+1)^{2}+4\end{aligned}
$$

If we plug the above into our integral and collect a factor of ${\color{red}{4}}$, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}+2x+5}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x+1)^{2}+4}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{4[(x+1)^{2}/4+1]}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{(x+1)^{2}/4+1}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+(x+1)^{2}/4}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((x+1)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Notice that this now resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{1+u^2} \, \textrm{d}u = \arctan u + C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $\left(\dfrac{x+1}{2}\right).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\dfrac{x+1}{2}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = 2\,\textrm{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}+2x+5}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((x+1)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arctan}u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arctan}(\frac{x+1}{2})+C\end{aligned}
$$

And we're done!


QUESTION 17592186069024
$\int \frac{1}{(2x-3)^{2}+4}\,\text{d}x=$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(2x-3)^{2}+4}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{4((2x-3)^{2}/4+1)}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+(2x-3)^{2}/4}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((2x-3)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{2x-3}{2}).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=\frac{2x-3}{2}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(2x-3)^{2}+4}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((2x-3)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{arctan}⁡(\frac{2x-3}{2})+C\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186250321
$\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4-{(6x+1)}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x=$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4-(6x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4(1-(6x+1)^{2}/4)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4}\cdot \sqrt{1-(6x+1)^{2}/4}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-(6x+1)^{2}/4}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(6x+1)^{2}/4}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((6x+1)/2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{6x+1}{2}).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=\frac{6x+1}{2}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=3\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{3}\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{4-(6x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((6x+1)/2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot \frac{1}{3}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{6}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{6}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{6x+1}{2})+C\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186250322
$\int \frac{2}{|x-3|\sqrt{(x-3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x=$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{2}{|x-3|\sqrt{(x-3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =2\int \frac{1}{|x-3|\sqrt{(x-3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}(u)+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x-3).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=x-3.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{2}{|x-3|\sqrt{(x-3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =2\int \frac{1}{|x-3|\sqrt{(x-3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =2\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(u)+C \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(x-3)+C\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186250323
$\int \frac{4}{|2x-7|\sqrt{(2x-7)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x=$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{4}{|2x-7|\sqrt{(2x-7)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =4\int \frac{1}{|2x-7|\sqrt{(2x-7)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}(u)+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(2x-7).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=2x-7.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=2\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{4}{|2x-7|\sqrt{(2x-7)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =4\int \frac{1}{|2x-7|\sqrt{(2x-7)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =4\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\cdot \frac{1}{2}\text{d}u \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(u)+C \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(2x-7)+C\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186348359
$\int \frac{2}{|x+1|\sqrt{(x+1)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x=$

Let's rewrite our integral as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{2}{|x+1|\sqrt{(x+1)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =2\int \frac{1}{|x+1|\sqrt{(x+1)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}(u)+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+1).$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=x+1.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{2}{|x+1|\sqrt{(x+1)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x & =2\int \frac{1}{|x+1|\sqrt{(x+1)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =2\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(u)+C \\  & =2\,\text{arcsec}(x+1)+C\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186069282
$\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-4x+7}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-4x+7 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+({2}^{2}-{2}^{2})+7 \\  & =({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})-4+7 \\  & =(x-2)^{2}+3\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-4x+7}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x-2)^{2}+3}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-2)/\sqrt{3})^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C.
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x-2}{\sqrt{3}}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x-2}{\sqrt{3}}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{3}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-4x+7}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-2)/\sqrt{3})^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\cdot \sqrt{3}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\operatorname{arctan}⁡(\frac{x-2}{\sqrt{3}})+C\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186069400
Evaluate the integral $\displaystyle \int_{-2}^{\sqrt{3} - 3}\frac{8}{x^{2} + 6x + 10}dx$.




QUESTION 17592186250320
$\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-6x+18}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-6x+18 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+({3}^{2}-{3}^{2})+18 \\  & =({x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2})-9+18 \\  & =(x-3)^{2}+9\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-6x+18}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x-3)^{2}+9}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-3)/3)^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C.
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x-3}{3}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x-3}{3}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{3}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=3\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-6x+18}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-3)/3)^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\cdot 3\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arctan}⁡(\frac{x-3}{3})+C\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186287952
Evaluate the integral ${\int }_{3}^{3+\sqrt{3}}\frac{5}{{x}^{2}-6x+10}\,\text{d}x.$

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-6x+10 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+({3}^{2}-{3}^{2})+10 \\  & =({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2})-9+10 \\  & =(x-3)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{5}{{x}^{2}-6x+10}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{5}{(x-3)^{2}+1}\,\text{d}x \\  & =5\int \frac{1}{1+(x-3)^{2}}\,\text{d}x\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x-3).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x-3.
$$

 
Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $3$ | $3+\sqrt{3}$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $0$ | $\sqrt{3}$ |

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{3}^{3+\sqrt{3}}\frac{5}{{x}^{2}-6x+10}\,\text{d}x & =5{\int }_{3}^{3+\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1+(x-3)^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =5{\int }_{0}^{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =5\operatorname{arctan}⁡u\,{|}_{0}^{\sqrt{3}} \\  & =5(\operatorname{arctan}⁡\sqrt{3}-\operatorname{arctan}⁡0) \\  & =5(\frac{\pi }{3}-0) \\  & =5(\frac{\pi }{3}) \\  & =\frac{5\pi }{3}.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186319263
Evaluate the following integral. Do not round the answer.

${\int }_{-3}^{\sqrt{3}-3}\frac{6}{{x}^{2}+6x+10}\,\text{d}x=$
{{field-1}}

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+6x+10 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+({3}^{2}-{3}^{2})+10 \\  & =({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2})-9+10 \\  & =(x+3)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{6}{{x}^{2}+6x+10}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{6}{(x+3)^{2}+1}\,\text{d}x \\  & =6\int \frac{1}{1+(x+3)^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+3).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+3.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-3$ | $\sqrt{3}-3$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $0$ | $\sqrt{3}$ |

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-3}^{\sqrt{3}-3}\frac{6}{{x}^{2}+6x+10}\,\text{d}x & =6{\int }_{0}^{\sqrt{3}}\frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =6\operatorname{arctan}⁡u\,{|}_{0}^{\sqrt{3}} \\  & =6(\operatorname{arctan}⁡\sqrt{3}-\operatorname{arctan}⁡(0)) \\  & =6(\frac{\pi }{3}-0) \\  & =2\pi \end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186319264
Evaluate the following integral. Do not round the answer.

${\int }_{1}^{3}\frac{6}{{x}^{2}-4x+5}\,\text{d}x=$
{{field-1}}

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-4x+5 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+({2}^{2}-{2}^{2})+5 \\  & =({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})-4+5 \\  & =(x-2)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{6}{{x}^{2}-4x+5}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{6}{(x-2)^{2}+1}\,\text{d}x \\  & =6\int \frac{1}{1+(x-2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x-2).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x-2.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $1$ | $3$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $-1$ | $1$ |

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{1}^{3}\frac{6}{{x}^{2}-4x+5}\,\text{d}x & =6{\int }_{-1}^{1}\frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =6\operatorname{arctan}⁡u\,{|}_{-1}^{1} \\  & =6(\operatorname{arctan}⁡(1)-\operatorname{arctan}⁡(-1)) \\  & =6(\frac{\pi }{4}-(-\frac{\pi }{4})) \\  & =6(\frac{\pi }{2}) \\  & =3\pi \end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186323726
$\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-8x+20}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-8x+20 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 4x+({4}^{2}-{4}^{2})+20 \\  & =({x}^{2}-2\cdot 4x+{4}^{2})-16+20 \\  & =(x-4)^{2}+4\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-8x+20}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x-4)^{2}+4}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-4)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse tangent:

$$
\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C.
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x-4}{2}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x-4}{2}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{{x}^{2}-8x+20}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+((x-4)/2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1+{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arctan}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arctan}⁡(\frac{x-4}{2})+C\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186069171
Evaluate the integral ${\int }_{-13/2}^{-11/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-12x-35}}\,\text{d}x.$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}-12x-35 & =-({x}^{2}+12x+35) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 6x+[{6}^{2}-{6}^{2}]+35) \\  & =-((x+6)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(x+6)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
{\int }_{-13/2}^{-11/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-12x-35}}\,\text{d}x={\int }_{-13/2}^{-11/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+6)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $x+6.$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+6.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-13/2$ | $-11/2$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $-1/2$ | $1/2$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-13/2}^{-11/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-12x-35}}\,\text{d}x & ={\int }_{-13/2}^{-11/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+6)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & ={\int }_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u\,{|}_{-1/2}^{1/2} \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{1}{2})-\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(-\frac{1}{2}) \\  & =\frac{\pi }{6}-(-\frac{\pi }{6}) \\  & =\frac{\pi }{3}.\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186292695
Evaluate the integral ${\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{1-2x-{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}1-2x-{x}^{2} & =1-({x}^{2}+2x) \\  & =1-({x}^{2}+2x+1-1) \\  & =1-((x+1)^{2}-1) \\  & =2-(x+1)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{1-2x-{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & ={\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{2-(x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & ={\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{2(1-(x+1)^{2}/2)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & ={\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{2}\cdot \sqrt{1-(x+1)^{2}/(\sqrt{2})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{14}{\sqrt{2}}{\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+1)/\sqrt{2})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x+1}{\sqrt{2}}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=(\frac{x+1}{\sqrt{2}}).
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{2}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-1-\sqrt{2}/2$ | $-1+\sqrt{2}/2$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $-1/2$ | $1/2$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{14}{\sqrt{1+2x-{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{14}{\sqrt{2}}{\int }_{-1-\sqrt{2}/2}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+1)/\sqrt{2})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{14}{\sqrt{2}}{\int }_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot \sqrt{2}\,\text{d}u. \\  & =14{\int }_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =14\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u\,{|}_{-1/2}^{1/2} \\  & =14(\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{1}{2})-\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(-\frac{1}{2})) \\  & =14(\frac{\pi }{6}-(-\frac{\pi }{6})) \\  & =\frac{14\pi }{3}.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186292697
Evaluate the integral ${\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{-12-x(x+12)}}\,\text{d}x.$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-12-x(x+12) & =-12-({x}^{2}+12x) \\  & =-12-({x}^{2}+12x+36-36) \\  & =-12-((x+6)^{2}-36) \\  & =24-(x+6)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{-12-x(x+12)}}\,\text{d}x & ={\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{24-(x+6)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & ={\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{24(1-(x+6)^{2}/24)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & ={\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{24}\cdot \sqrt{1-(x+6)^{2}/(2\sqrt{6})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{15}{2\sqrt{6}}{\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+6)/2\sqrt{6})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x+6}{2\sqrt{6}}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x+6}{2\sqrt{6}}.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\sqrt{6}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-6-\sqrt{6}$ | $-6+\sqrt{6}$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $-\frac{1}{2}$ | $\frac{1}{2}$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{15}{\sqrt{-12-x(x+12)}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{15}{2\sqrt{6}}{\int }_{-6-\sqrt{6}}^{-6+\sqrt{6}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+6)/2\sqrt{6})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{15}{2\sqrt{6}}{\int }_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot 2\sqrt{6}\,\text{d}u. \\  & =15{\int }_{-1/2}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =15\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u\,{|}_{-1/2}^{1/2} \\  & =15(\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{1}{2})-\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(-\frac{1}{2})) \\  & =15(\frac{\pi }{6}-(-\frac{\pi }{6})) \\  & =5\pi .\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+1).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+1.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-1$ | $-1/2$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $0$ | $1/2$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-1}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-x(x+2)}}\,\text{d}x & ={\int }_{0}^{1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u\,{|}_{0}^{1/2} \\  & =arcsin\,(\frac{1}{2})-arcsin\,(0) \\  & =\frac{\pi }{6}-0 \\  & =\frac{\pi }{6}.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186319261
Evaluate the following integral. Do not round the answer.

${\int }_{-1}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-2x}}\,\text{d}x=$
{{field-1}}

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}-2x & =-({x}^{2}+2x) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 1x+[{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}]) \\  & =-((x+1)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(x+1)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
{\int }_{-1}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-2x}}\,\text{d}x={\int }_{-1}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+1).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+1.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $-1$ | $-1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $0$ | $\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{-1}^{-1+\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-2x}}\,\text{d}x & ={\int }_{0}^{\sqrt{2}/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u\,{|}_{0}^{\sqrt{2}/2} \\  & =arcsin\,(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2})-\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(0) \\  & =\frac{\pi }{4}-0 \\  & =\frac{\pi }{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
{\int }_{-10}^{-8}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-18x-80}}\,\text{d}x={\int }_{-10}^{-8}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+9)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+9).$

So, let's perform the substitution

u=x+9.

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

QUESTION 17592186323731
$\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-8x-15}}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}-8x-15 & =-({x}^{2}+8x+15) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4x+[{4}^{2}-{4}^{2}]+15) \\  & =-((x+4)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(x+4)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-8x-15}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+4)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+4).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+4.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-8x-15}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-(x+4)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(x+4)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186332757
$\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{7+6x-{x}^{2}}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}7+6x-{x}^{2} & =-({x}^{2}-6x-7) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+[{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}]-7) \\  & =-((x-3)^{2}-16) \\  & =16-(x-3)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{7+6x-{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{16-(x-3)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{16(1-(x-3)^{2}/16)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{16}\cdot \sqrt{1-(x-3)^{2}/16}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{4\sqrt{1-(x-3)^{2}/16}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x-3)/4)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x-3}{4}).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x-3}{4}.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{4}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=4\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{7+6x-{x}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x-3)/4)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot 4\,\text{d}u \\  & =\int \frac{\text{d}u}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}} \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{x-3}{4})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186332789
$\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-3{x}^{2}-18x+6}}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-3{x}^{2}-18x+6 & =-3({x}^{2}+6x-2) \\  & =-3({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+[{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}]-2) \\  & =-3({(x+3)}^{2}-11) \\  & =3(11-{(x+3)}^{2})\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-3{x}^{2}-18x+6}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{3(11-{(x+3)}^{2})}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{33(1-(x+3)^{2}/11)}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{33}\cdot \sqrt{1-(x+3)^{2}/11}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{33}}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+3)/\sqrt{11})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse sine:

$$
\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u=\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(\frac{x+3}{\sqrt{11}}).$  

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=\frac{x+3}{\sqrt{11}}.
$$

Differentiating, we get 

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{11}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{11}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{-3{x}^{2}-18x+6}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{33}}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-((x+3)/\sqrt{11})^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{33}}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot \sqrt{11}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\int \frac{1}{\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\operatorname{arcsin}⁡(\frac{x+3}{\sqrt{11}})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186250324
$\int \frac{1}{|x-4|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-8x+15}}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the expression inside the square root:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-8x+15 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 4x+({4}^{2}-{4}^{2})+15 \\  & =({x}^{2}-8x+16)-16+15 \\  & =(x-4)^{2}-1.\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{|x-4|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-8x+15}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{|x-4|\sqrt{(x-4)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}\,u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $x-4.$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=x-4.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{|x-4|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-8x+15}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{|x-4|\sqrt{(x-4)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\text{arcsec}\,u+C \\  & =\text{arcsec}(x-4)+C.\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186319256
Evaluate the following integral. Do not round the answer.

${\int }_{\sqrt{2}-3}^{-1}\frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+6x+8}}\,\text{d}x=$
{{field-1}}

First, we complete the square for the expression inside the square root:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+6x+8 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+({3}^{2}-{3}^{2})+8 \\  & =({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2})-9+8 \\  & =(x+3)^{2}-1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
{\int }_{\sqrt{2}-3}^{-1}\frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+6x+8}}\,\text{d}x=6{\int }_{\sqrt{2}-3}^{-1}\frac{1}{|x+3|\sqrt{(x+3)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}\,u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $(x+3).$

So, let's perform the substitution

$$
u=x+3.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Let's use the table below to change the limits from $x$ to $u.$

| $x$ | $\sqrt{2}-3$ | $-1$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| $u$ | $\sqrt{2}$ | $2$ |

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}{\int }_{\sqrt{2}-3}^{-1}\frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+6x+8}}\,\text{d}x & =6{\int }_{\sqrt{2}}^{2}\frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =6\,\text{arcsec}\,u\,{|}_{\sqrt{2}}^{2} \\  & =6(\text{arcsec}(2)-\text{arcsec}(\sqrt{2})) \\  & =6(\frac{\pi }{3}-\frac{\pi }{4}) \\  & =\frac{\pi }{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186332764
$\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\sqrt{(x+2)^{2}-25}}\,\text{d}x=$

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+2)^{2}-25 & =25[\frac{(x+2)^{2}}{25}-1] \\  & =25[{(\frac{x+2}{5})}^{2}-1]\end{aligned}

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\sqrt{(x+2)^{2}-25}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\sqrt{25[((x+2)/5)^{2}-1]}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\cdot \sqrt{25}\cdot \sqrt{((x+2)/5)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\cdot 5\cdot \sqrt{((x+2)/5)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{15}{|x+2|\sqrt{((x+2)/5)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =3\int \frac{1}{|(x+2)/5|\sqrt{((x+2)/5)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}\,u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $\frac{x+2}{5}.$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=\frac{x+2}{5}.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{5}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=5\,\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{75}{|x+2|\sqrt{(x+2)^{2}-25}}\,\text{d}x & =3\int \frac{1}{|(x+2)/5|\sqrt{((x+2)/5)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =3\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\cdot 5\,\text{d}u \\  & =15\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =15\,\text{arcsec }u+C \\  & =15\,\text{arcsec}(\frac{x+2}{5})+C.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186390353
Calculate $\int \frac{10}{|x-6|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-12x+35}}\,\text{d}x.$

First, we complete the square for the expression inside the square root:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-12x+35 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 6x+({6}^{2}-{6}^{2})+35 \\  & =({x}^{2}-12x+36)-36+35 \\  & =(x-6)^{2}-1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above in the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{10}{|x-6|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-12x+35}}\,\text{d}x=10\int \frac{1}{|x-6|\sqrt{(x-6)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

This resembles the basic integral for inverse secant:

$$
\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u=\text{arcsec}\,u+C
$$

The only difference is that instead of $u$ as the variable, we have $x-6.$

So, let's perform the substitution 

$$
u=x-6.
$$

Differentiating, we get

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Finally, we write the integral in terms of $u,$ and evaluate:

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{10}{|x-6|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-12x+35}}\,\text{d}x & =10\int \frac{1}{|x-6|\sqrt{(x-6)^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =10\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{{u}^{2}-1}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =10\,\text{arcsec }u+C \\  & =10\,\text{arcsec}(x-6)+C.\end{aligned}
$$


LESSON Determining Circle Properties by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186078247
Find the center and radius of the circle whose equation is $x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 6y = 12.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-4x+6y & =12 \\ ({x}^{2}-4x)+({y}^{2}+6y) & =12 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}) & =12+{2}^{2}+{3}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}) & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-2)^{2}+(y+3)^{2} & ={5}^{2} \\ (x-2)^{2}+(y-(-3))^{2} & ={5}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(2,-3)$ and its radius is $5.$


EXAMPLE 17592186078248
Find the center and radius of the circle whose equation is $x^2 + y^2 - x  = 6.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-x & =6 \\ ({x}^{2}-x)+{y}^{2} & =6 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+{y}^{2} & =6+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+{y}^{2} & =\frac{25}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{y}^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2} \\ {(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+(y-0)^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $\left(\dfrac{1}{2}, 0 \right)$ and its radius is $\dfrac{5}{2}.$


TUTORIAL 17592186079635 Introduction
Consider the equation 

$$
x^2 +  y^2 - 2x - 4y = 4.
$$

Believe it or not, this equation actually represents a circle! Its graph is given below:

![](images/5d/5dfea5042d6c84f40a2790a01ee6aafb64433f7422ce88b589cf670d93771e2c.png)

   

Clearly, this is a circle of radius $3$ centered at $(1,2).$ But how can we determine the center and radius from the given circle equation?

Let's start with the equation

$$
x^2 +  y^2 - 2x - 4y = 4.
$$

By grouping the $x$ and $y$ terms and completing the squares, we can write the equation in the standard form

$$
(x-a)^2 + (y-b)^2 = r^2.
$$

We carry out the process with the following steps:

**Step 1. **Group the $x$ and $y$ terms.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-2x-4y & =4 \\ ({x}^{2}-2x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =4\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 2.** In each group, add the square of half the linear coefficient to both sides of the equation. 

- Here, the $x$ coefficient is $-2,$ so we add $\left( \dfrac{-2}{2} \right)^2 = (-1)^2 = 1$ to both sides.

- Likewise, the $y$ coefficient is $-4,$ so we add $\left( \dfrac{-4}{2} \right)^2 = (-2)^2 = 4$ to both sides.

So, we have

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}-2x+1)+({y}^{2}-4y+4) & =4+1+4 \\ ({x}^{2}-2x+1)+({y}^{2}-4y+4) & =9.\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 3.** Finally, we factor each group into a perfect square, and we get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & =9 \\ (x-1)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & ={3}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can see that the equation represents a circle centered at $(1,2)$ with radius $3.$


QUESTION 17592186079061
Find the center and radius of the circle whose equation is ${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+4x-6y=3.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+4x-6y & =3 \\ ({x}^{2}+4x)+({y}^{2}-6y) & =3 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}) & =3+{2}^{2}+{3}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+4)+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 3y+9) & =16\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+2)^{2}+(y-3)^{2} & ={4}^{2} \\ (x-(-2))^{2}+(y-3)^{2} & ={4}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-2,3)$ and its radius is $4.$

QUESTION 17592186275313
A circle in the Cartesian plane is given by the equation $8x+{x}^{2}-2y=64-{y}^{2}.$ If the circle is written in the standard form $(x-a)^{2}+(y-b)^{2}={r}^{2},$ then $a+b+r=$

To find the standard form of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}8x+{x}^{2}-2y & =64-{y}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+8x)+({y}^{2}-2y) & =64 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4x+{4}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot y+{1}^{2}) & =64+{4}^{2}+{1}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4x+{4}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot y+{1}^{2}) & =81\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+4)^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & ={9}^{2} \\ (x-(-4))^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & ={9}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $a=-4,b=1$ and $r=9,$ and

$$
a+b+r=-4+1+9=6.
$$


QUESTION 17592186275326
A circle in the Cartesian plane is given by the equation 
$137+6y=-{y}^{2}-{x}^{2}-24x.$ If the circle is written in the standard form $(x-a)^{2}+(y-b)^{2}={r}^{2},$ then $a+b+r=$

To find the standard form of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}137+6y & =-{y}^{2}-{x}^{2}-24x \\ {x}^{2}+24x+{y}^{2}+6y & =-137 \\ ({x}^{2}+24x)+({y}^{2}+6y) & =-137 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 12x+{12}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}) & =-137+{12}^{2}+{3}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 12x+{12}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}) & =16\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+12)^{2}+(y+3)^{2} & ={4}^{2} \\ (x-(-12))^{2}+(y-(-3))^{2} & ={4}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $a=-12,b=-3$ and $r=4,$  and

$$
a+b+r=-12-3+4=-11.
$$


QUESTION 17592186275328
A circle in the Cartesian plane is given by the equation ${y}^{2}+2x+{x}^{2}=24y-120.$ If the circle is written in the standard form $(x-a)^{2}+(y-b)^{2}={r}^{2},$ then $a+b+r=$

To find the standard form $(x-a)^{2}+(y-b)^{2}={r}^{2},$ we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}+2x+{x}^{2} & =24y-120 \\ {x}^{2}+2x+{y}^{2}-24y & =-120 \\ ({x}^{2}+2x)+({y}^{2}-24y) & =-120 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 12y+{12}^{2}) & =-120+{1}^{2}+{12}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 12y+{12}^{2}) & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+1)^{2}+(y-12)^{2} & ={5}^{2} \\ (x-(-1))^{2}+(y-12)^{2} & ={5}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $a=-1,b=12,r=5,$ and 

$$
a+b+r=-1+12+5=16.
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}8x+32y+{y}^{2} & =-263-{x}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+8x)+({y}^{2}+32y) & =-263 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4x+{4}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 16y+{16}^{2}) & =-263+{4}^{2}+{16}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4x+{4}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 16y+{16}^{2}) & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+4)^{2}+(y+16)^{2} & ={3}^{2} \\ (x-(-4))^{2}+(y-(-16))^{2} & ={3}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-4,-16)$ and its radius is $3.$

QUESTION 17592186079067
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-3y=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-3y & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+({y}^{2}-3y) & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}y+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}) & ={(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{(y-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} \\ (x-0)^{2}+{(y-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(0,\frac{3}{2})$ and its radius is $\frac{3}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186079203

${x}^{2}+5x+{y}^{2}-2y=\frac{7}{4}.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+5x+{y}^{2}-2y & =\frac{7}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}+5x)+({y}^{2}-2y) & =\frac{7}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 1y+{1}^{2}) & =\frac{7}{4}+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+{1}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 1y+{1}^{2}) & =9.\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & ={3}^{2} \\ {(x-(-\frac{5}{2}))}^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & ={3}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-\frac{5}{2},1)$ and its radius is $3.$


QUESTION 17592186238070
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+3x-y-2=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+3x-y-2 & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}+3x)+({y}^{2}-y) & =2 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}y+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}) & =2+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}y+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}) & =\frac{9}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{9}{2} \\ {(x-(-\frac{3}{2}))}^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}})}^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})$ and its radius is $\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}.$


QUESTION 17592186238071
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-3x-4y=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-3x-4y & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}-3x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & ={(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+{2}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =\frac{25}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(\frac{3}{2},2)$ and its radius is $\frac{5}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186238072
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y=\frac{1}{2}.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}^{2}+({y}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y) & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}^{2}+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{4}y+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}) & ={(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}^{2}+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{4}y+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}) & =\frac{9}{16}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{4})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{4})}^{2} \\ (x-0)^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{4})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(0,\frac{1}{4})$ and its radius is $\frac{3}{4}.$


QUESTION 17592186275155
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+5x-3y-8=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+5x-3y-8 & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}+5x)+({y}^{2}-3y) & =8 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}y+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}) & =8+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}y+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}) & =\frac{33}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+{(y-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{33}{2} \\ {(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+{(y-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & ={(\sqrt{\frac{33}{2}})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-\frac{5}{2},\frac{3}{2})$ and its radius is $\sqrt{\frac{33}{2}}.$


QUESTION 17592186345210
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}-x+{y}^{2}-4y=-2.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-x+{y}^{2}-4y & =-2 \\ ({x}^{2}-x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =-2 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =-2+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{2}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =\frac{9}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{(y-2)}^{2} & =\frac{9}{4} \\ {(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{(y-2)}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(\frac{1}{2},2)$ and its radius is $\frac{3}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186345211
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+3x+{y}^{2}-y=-\frac{9}{4}.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+3x+{y}^{2}-y & =-\frac{9}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}+3x)+({y}^{2}-y) & =-\frac{9}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}y+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}) & =-\frac{9}{4}+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}y+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}) & =\frac{1}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} \\ {(x-(-\frac{3}{2}))}^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2})$ and its radius is $\frac{1}{2}$


QUESTION 17592186396066
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
$2{x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}-8x+8y+3=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}-8x+8y+3 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-4x+4y & =-\frac{3}{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-4x)+({y}^{2}+4y) & =-\frac{3}{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2})+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =-\frac{3}{2}+{2}^{2}+{2}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+4)+({y}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+4) & =\frac{13}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-2)^{2}+{(y+2)}^{2} & =\frac{13}{2} \\ (x-2)^{2}+{(y-(-2))}^{2} & ={(\sqrt{\frac{13}{2}})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(2,-2)$ and its radius is $\sqrt{\frac{13}{2}}.$


QUESTION 17592186399412
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+7x=0.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+7x & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}+7x)+{y}^{2} & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{7}{2}x+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2})+{y}^{2} & ={(\frac{7}{2})}^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{7}{2})}^{2}+{y}^{2} & ={(\frac{7}{2})}^{2} \\ {(x-(-\frac{7}{2}))}^{2}+(y-0)^{2} & ={(\frac{7}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-\frac{7}{2},0)$ and its radius is $\frac{7}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186403836
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
$2{x}^{2}-4x+2{y}^{2}-y=-1.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}-4x+2{y}^{2}-y & =-1 \\ {x}^{2}-2x+{y}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y & =-\frac{1}{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2x)+({y}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}y) & =-\frac{1}{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{4}y+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}) & =-\frac{1}{2}+{1}^{2}+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{4}y+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}) & =\frac{9}{16}\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{9}{16} \\ (x-1)^{2}+{(y-\frac{1}{4})}^{2} & ={(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(1,\frac{1}{4})$ and its radius is $\frac{3}{4}.$


QUESTION 17592186432241
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}-9x+18=4y-{y}^{2}.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-9x+18 & =4y-{y}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-9x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =-18 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{9}{2}x+{(\frac{9}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =-18+{(\frac{9}{2})}^{2}+{2}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{9}{2}x+{(\frac{9}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}) & =\frac{25}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{9}{2})}^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(\frac{9}{2},2)$ and its radius is $\frac{5}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186432242
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+7=7y-2x.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+7 & =7y-2x \\ ({x}^{2}+2x)+({y}^{2}-7y) & =-7 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{7}{2}y+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2}) & =-7+{1}^{2}+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2} \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{7}{2}y+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2}) & =\frac{25}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect squares and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+1)}^{2}+{(y-\frac{7}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2} \\ {(x-(-1))}^{2}+{(y-\frac{7}{2})}^{2} & ={(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(-1,\frac{7}{2})$ and its radius is $\frac{5}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186498585
Find the radius and center of the circle whose equation is
${x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-x-2y=\frac{11}{4}.$

To find the center and radius of the circle, we need to group $x$ and $y$ terms and complete the squares.

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-x-2y & =\frac{11}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}-x)+({y}^{2}-2y) & =\frac{11}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot y+{1}^{2}) & ={(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{1}^{2}+\frac{11}{4} \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2})+({y}^{2}-2\cdot y+{1}^{2}) & =4\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we can factor the perfect square and get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+{(y-1)}^{2} & ={2}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the circle is centered at $(\frac{1}{2},1)$ and its radius is $2.$


LESSON Calculating the Vertex of a Parabola by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186171811
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $y^2 - 8x - 6y + 33 = 0$.

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^2 = 4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is a constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}-8x-6y+33 & =0 \\ {y}^{2}-6y & =8x-33 \\ (y-3)^{2}-9 & =8x-33 \\ (y-3)^{2} & =8x-33+9 \\ (y-3)^{2} & =8x-24 \\ (y-3)^{2} & =8(x-3) \\ (y-3)^{2} & =4(2)(x-3)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k) = (3,3).$


EXAMPLE 17592186171824
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $5x^2 -10x - 4y - 7 = 0.$

The standard equation of an upward- or downward-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(x-h)^2 = 4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is a constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}-10x-4y-7 & =0 \\ 5{x}^{2}-10x & =4y+7 \\ 5[{x}^{2}-2x] & =4y+7 \\ 5[(x-1)^{2}-1] & =4y+7 \\ 5(x-1)^{2}-5 & =4y+7 \\ 5(x-1)^{2} & =4y+7+5 \\ 5(x-1)^{2} & =4y+12 \\ 5(x-1)^{2} & =4(y+3) \\ (x-1)^{2} & =\frac{4}{5}(y+3) \\ (x-1)^{2} & =4(\frac{1}{5})(y+3)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k) = (1,-3).$


TUTORIAL 17592186173147 Introduction
Suppose we are given the parabola 

$$
x^2 - 4x - 8y - 28 = 0,
$$

whose graph is shown below. How can we find the vertex of the parabola?

![](images/28/2849c732102f52d5f7f2e0f5e29724560d4d7052df80e90fe8d24b95911f5092.png)

Recall that the standard equation of a *vertical* parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(x-h)^2 = 4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is a constant. 

To write our equation in standard form, we group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-4x-8y-28 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}-4x & =8y+28 \\ (x-2)^{2}-4 & =8y+28 \\ (x-2)^{2} & =8y+32 \\ (x-2)^{2} & =8(y+4) \\ (x-2)^{2} & =4(2)(y-(-4))\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k) = ({\color{blue}2},{\color{red}-4}).$

Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $y^{2} - 8x - 4y - 12 = 0$.

Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $3y^{2} + 4x - 6y - 1 = 0$.




QUESTION 17592186238037
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola ${y}^{2}-8x+6y+49=0.$

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^{2}=4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}-8x+6y+49 & =0 \\ {y}^{2}+6y & =8x-49 \\ (y+3)^{2}-9 & =8x-49 \\ (y+3)^{2} & =8x-49+9 \\ (y+3)^{2} & =8x-40 \\ (y+3)^{2} & =8(x-5) \\ (y+3)^{2} & =4(2)(x-5)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(5,-3).$


QUESTION 17592186238038
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $2{y}^{2}-x+4y=0.$

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^{2}=4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{y}^{2}-x+4y & =0 \\ 2{y}^{2}+4y & =x \\ 2[{y}^{2}+2y] & =x \\ 2[(y+1)^{2}-1] & =x \\ 2(y+1)^{2}-2 & =x \\ 2(y+1)^{2} & =x+2 \\ (y+1)^{2} & =\frac{1}{2}(x+2) \\ (y+1)^{2} & =4(\frac{1}{8})(x+2)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(-2,-1).$


QUESTION 17592186238039
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola ${y}^{2}-8x-2y+33=0.$

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^{2}=4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}-8x-2y+33 & =0 \\ {y}^{2}-2y & =8x-33 \\ (y-1)^{2}-1 & =8x-33 \\ (y-1)^{2} & =8x-33+1 \\ (y-1)^{2} & =8x-32 \\ (y-1)^{2} & =8(x-4) \\ (y-1)^{2} & =4(2)(x-4)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(4,1).$

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^{2}=4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}-4x-4y+24 & =0 \\ {y}^{2}-4y & =4x-24 \\ (y-2)^{2}-4 & =4x-24 \\ (y-2)^{2} & =4x-24+4 \\ (y-2)^{2} & =4x-20 \\ (y-2)^{2} & =4(x-5) \\ (y-2)^{2} & =4(1)(x-5)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(5,2).$


QUESTION 17592186354477
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $3{y}^{2}-2x+18y+29=0.$

The standard equation of a left- or right-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(y-k)^{2}=4p(x-h),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $y$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{y}^{2}-2x+18y+29 & =0 \\ 3{y}^{2}+18y & =2x-29 \\ 3[{y}^{2}+6y] & =2x-29 \\ 3[(y+3)^{2}-9] & =2x-29 \\ 3(y+3)^{2}-27 & =2x-29 \\ 3(y+3)^{2} & =2x-29+27 \\ 3(y+3)^{2} & =2x-2 \\ 3(y+3)^{2} & =2(x-1) \\ (y+3)^{2} & =\frac{2}{3}(x-1) \\ (y+3)^{2} & =4(\frac{1}{6})(x-1)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(1,-3).$

QUESTION 17592186172274
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $3{x}^{2}+6x+4y+15=0.$

The standard equation of an upward- or downward-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(x-h)^{2}=4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}+6x+4y+15 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}+6x & =-4y-15 \\ 3[{x}^{2}+2x] & =-4y-15 \\ 3[(x+1)^{2}-1] & =-4y-15 \\ 3(x+1)^{2}-3 & =-4y-15 \\ 3(x+1)^{2} & =-4y-15+3 \\ 3(x+1)^{2} & =-4y-12 \\ 3(x+1)^{2} & =-4(y+3) \\ (x+1)^{2} & =-\frac{4}{3}(y+3) \\ (x+1)^{2} & =4(-\frac{1}{3})(y+3)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(-1,-3).$

$$
(x-h)^{2}=4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2x-2y-1 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+2x & =2y+1 \\ (x+1)^{2}-1 & =2y+1 \\ (x+1)^{2} & =2y+1+1 \\ (x+1)^{2} & =2y+2 \\ (x+1)^{2} & =4(\frac{1}{2})(y+1)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(-1,-1).$

$$

QUESTION 17592186253518

The standard equation of an upward- or downward-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(x-h)^{2}=4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}-30x-8y+67 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}-30x & =8y-67 \\ 3[{x}^{2}-10x] & =8y-67 \\ 3[(x-5)^{2}-25] & =8y-67 \\ 3(x-5)^{2}-75 & =8y-67 \\ 3(x-5)^{2} & =8y-67+75 \\ 3(x-5)^{2} & =8y+8 \\ 3(x-5)^{2} & =8(y+1) \\ (x-5)^{2} & =\frac{8}{3}(y+1) \\ (x-5)^{2} & =4(\frac{2}{3})(y+1)\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186354395
Calculate the coordinates of the vertex of the parabola $5{x}^{2}+30x+2y+41=0.$

The standard equation of an upward- or downward-opening parabola that has its vertex at $(h,k)$ is

$$
(x-h)^{2}=4p(y-k),
$$

where $p$ is constant.

To write the given equation in this form, we need to group the $x$-terms on the left-hand side and complete the square, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}+30x+2y+41 & =0 \\ 5{x}^{2}+30x & =-2y-41 \\ 5[{x}^{2}+6x] & =-2y-41 \\ 5[(x+3)^{2}-9] & =-2y-41 \\ 5(x+3)^{2}-45 & =-2y-41 \\ 5(x+3)^{2} & =-2y-41+45 \\ 5(x+3)^{2} & =-2y+4 \\ 5(x+3)^{2} & =-2(y-2) \\ (x+3)^{2} & =-\frac{2}{5}(y-2) \\ (x+3)^{2} & =4(-\frac{1}{10})(y-2)\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the coordinates of the vertex are $(h,k)=(-3,2).$


LESSON Integration Using Inverse Reciprocal Hyperbolic Functions and Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186166644
${\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{(x-1)\sqrt{2x-x^2}} \textrm{d}x= }$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\eqalign{
2x-x^2 & = -(x^2 - 2x)\[5pt]
& = -(x^2 - 2\cdot x + 1^2-1^2)\[5pt]
& = -((x-1)^2 - 1) \[5pt]
&= 1 - (x-1)^2
}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{(x-1)\sqrt{2x-x^2}} \textrm{d}x  = \int  \dfrac{1}{(x-1)\sqrt { 1-(x-1)^2 } }	\, \textrm{d}x.
$$

Let $u = x-1.$ Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(x-1)\sqrt{2x-{x}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x-1)\sqrt{1-(x-1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\text{d}u \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡(x-1)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186166645
Calculate $\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{4-(2x-2)^2} \textrm{d}x.$

First, we note that

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{4-(2x-2)^2}\textrm{d}x = 
\int \dfrac{1}{4-4(x-1)^2}\textrm{d}x = 
\dfrac{1}{4} {\int}\dfrac{1}{ 1 - (x-1)^2 }\textrm{d}x.
$$

Let $u = x-1.$ Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(x-1)}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot \text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(x-1)+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $\left|{x-1} \right| >   1.$


EXAMPLE 17592186166646
${\displaystyle \int \dfrac{6}{|3x+9|\sqrt{x^2+6x+10}} \textrm{d}x= }$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+6x+10 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}+10 \\  & =(x+3)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{6}{|3x\!+\!9|\sqrt{x^2\!+\!6x\!+\!10}} \,\textrm{d}x  = \int  \dfrac{6}{3|x\!+\!3|\sqrt {1\!+\!(x\!+\!3)^2} }	\, \textrm{d}x = {2} \int \dfrac{1}{|x\!+\!3|\sqrt{1\!+\!(x\!+\!3)^2}} \, \textrm{d}x.
$$

Let $u = x+3.$ Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}2\int \frac{1}{|x+3|\sqrt{1+(x+3)^{2}}}\text{d}x & =2\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-2\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-2\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x+3)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186166647
$\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{3-2x-x^2} \textrm{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\eqalign{
3-2x-x^2 & = -(x^2 + 2x - 3)\[5pt]
& = -(x^2 + 2\cdot x +1^2 - 1^2 - 3)\[5pt]
& = -((x+1)^2 - 4) \[5pt]
&= 4 - (x+1)^2
}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{3-2x-x^2}\textrm{d}x =
\int \dfrac{1}{4-(x+1)^2}\textrm{d}x = 
\dfrac{1}{4} {\int}\dfrac{1}{ 1 - \left( \frac{1}{2}(x+1)\right)^2 }\textrm{d}x.
$$

Let $u = \dfrac{1}{2}(x+1).$ Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = 2\,\textrm{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{2}(x+1))}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{1}{2}(x+1))+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x+1}{2})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $\left|\dfrac {x+1} {2} \right| >   1.$


TUTORIAL 17592186168099 Introduction
We can also complete the square to integrate hyperbolic *arcsecant*, *arccosecant*, and *arccotangent*.

For example, let's consider the integral

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{\left(x+3\right)\sqrt{-x^2-6x}} \textrm{d}x.
$$

As it is, this integral doesn't resemble any of the basic integrals we have encountered so far. However, we can complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}-6x & =-({x}^{2}+6x) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}) \\  & =-((x+3)^{2}-9) \\  & =9-(x+3)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

If we plug the above into our integral and collect a factor of $9$ under the square root, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(x+3)\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-6x}}\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x+3)\sqrt{9-(x+3)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{3(x+3)\sqrt{1-{(\frac{x+3}{3})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{(\frac{x+3}{3})\sqrt{1-{(\frac{x+3}{3})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

This now looks more familiar! Let $u = \dfrac {x+3}{3}.$ Then

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{3}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\textrm{d}x = 3\,\textrm{d}u.
$$

We can now write the integral as

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(x+3)\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-6x}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{(\frac{x+3}{3})\sqrt{1-{(\frac{x+3}{3})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot (3\,\text{d}u) \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u.\end{aligned}
$$

This is a basic integral for an inverse reciprocal hyperbolic function, and we can solve it in one step:
$\newcommand{\arsinh}{\mathop{\rm arsinh}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\arcosh}{\mathop{\rm arcosh}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\artanh}{\mathop{\rm artanh}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\sech}{\mathop{\rm sech}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\csch}{\mathop{\rm csch}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\coth}{\mathop{\rm coth}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\arsech}{\mathop{\rm arsech}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\arcsch}{\mathop{\rm arcsch}\nolimits}
\newcommand{\arcoth}{\mathop{\rm arcoth}\nolimits}$

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arsech}⁡(\frac{x+3}{3})+C.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186167435
$\int \frac{1}{(x+2)\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-4x-3}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}-4x-3 & =-({x}^{2}+4x+3) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}+3) \\  & =-((x+2)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(x+2)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{(x+2)\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-4x-3}}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{(x+2)\sqrt{1-(x+2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=x+2.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(x+2)\sqrt{-{x}^{2}-4x-3}}\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{(x+2)\sqrt{1-(x+2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\text{d}u \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡(x+2)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186167518
$\int \frac{\sqrt{5}}{(x-1)\sqrt{4+2x-{x}^{2}}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}+2x+4 & =-({x}^{2}-2x-4) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}-4) \\  & =-((x-1)^{2}-5) \\  & =5-(x-1)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{\sqrt{5}}{(x-1)\sqrt{4+2x-{x}^{2}}} & =\int \frac{\sqrt{5}}{(x-1)\sqrt{5-{(x-1)}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})\sqrt{5-5{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\int \frac{1}{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})\sqrt{1-{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{5}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{\sqrt{5}}{(x-1)\sqrt{4+2x-{x}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\int \frac{1}{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})\sqrt{1-{(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\cdot \sqrt{5}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\operatorname{arsech}⁡(\frac{x-1}{\sqrt{5}})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186538968
$\int \frac{1}{(3x-15)\sqrt{10x-{x}^{2}-24}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}+10x-24 & =-({x}^{2}-10x+24) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot 5x+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2}+24) \\  & =-((x-5)^{2}-1) \\  & =1-(x-5)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{(3x-15)\sqrt{10x-{x}^{2}-24}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{(x-5)\sqrt{1-(x-5)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=x-5.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(3x-15)\sqrt{10x-{x}^{2}-24}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{(x-5)\sqrt{1-(x-5)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arsech}⁡(x-5)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186538975

$$
\begin{aligned}-4{x}^{2}-8x & =-4({x}^{2}+2x) \\  & =-4({x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}) \\  & =-4[(x+1)^{2}-1] \\  & =4[1-(x+1)^{2}]\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{(x+1)\sqrt{-4{x}^{2}-8x}}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{(x+1)\sqrt{4[1-(x+1)^{2}]}}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{(x+1)\sqrt{1-(x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{(x+1)\sqrt{-4{x}^{2}-8x}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{(x+1)\sqrt{1-(x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{u\sqrt{1-{u}^{2}}}\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arsech}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arsech}⁡(x+1)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186563720

$$

$$

$$

$$

$$

$$

$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$

QUESTION 17592186167433

$$

$$

$$


QUESTION 17592186167434
Calculate $\int \frac{\text{d}x}{|6x-2|\sqrt{9+(3x-1)^{2}}}.$

$$
\int \frac{\text{d}x}{|6x-2|\sqrt{9+(3x-1)^{2}}}=\int \frac{\text{d}x}{6|x-\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{9+9{(x-\frac{1}{3})}^{2}}}=\frac{1}{18}\int \frac{1}{|x-\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{1+{(x-\frac{1}{3})}^{2}}}\text{d}x.

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{18}\int \frac{1}{|x-\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{1+{(x-\frac{1}{3})}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{18}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{18}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{18}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x-\frac{1}{3})+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{18}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(\frac{3x-1}{3})+C.\end{aligned}
$$
