QUESTION 17592186539016
Calculate $\int \frac{1}{4-(x+3)^{2}}\text{d}x.$

First, we note that

$$
\int \frac{1}{4-(x+3)^{2}}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{4-4{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{x+3}{2}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x+3}{2})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{x+3}{2}|>1.$


QUESTION 17592186167436
$\int \frac{2}{|4x-8|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-4x+5}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-4x+5 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}+5 \\  & =(x-2)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{2}{|4x-8|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-4x+5}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{2}{4|x-2|\sqrt{1+(x-2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|x-2|\sqrt{1+(x-2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=x-2.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|x-2|\sqrt{1+(x-2)^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x-2)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186167519
$\int \frac{2}{|2x+1|\sqrt{4{x}^{2}+4x+5}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+4x+5 & =4({x}^{2}+x+\frac{5}{4}) \\  & =4({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+\frac{5}{4}) \\  & =4[{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+1]\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{2}{|2x+1|\sqrt{4{x}^{2}+4x+5}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{2}|\sqrt{4[1+{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}]}}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=x+\frac{1}{2}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x+\frac{1}{2})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186538970
$\int -\frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+6x+13}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+6x+13 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}+13 \\  & =(x+3)^{2}+4\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int -\frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+6x+13}}\,\text{d}x & =-\int \frac{6}{|x+3|\sqrt{4+(x+3)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =-\int \frac{6}{2|\frac{x+3}{2}|\sqrt{4+4{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =-\frac{3}{2}\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x+3}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=\frac{x+3}{2}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}-\frac{3}{2}\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x+3}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x+3}{2})}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =-\frac{3}{2}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =-3\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =3\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =3\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(\frac{x+3}{2})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186538980
$\int \frac{3}{|3x+1|\sqrt{9{x}^{2}+6x+10}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}9{x}^{2}+6x+10 & =9({x}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}x+\frac{10}{9}) \\  & =9({x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{3}x+{(\frac{1}{3})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{3})}^{2}+\frac{10}{9}) \\  & =9[{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}+1]\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{3}{|3x+1|\sqrt{9{x}^{2}+6x+10}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{9[1+{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}]}}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{1+{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=x+\frac{1}{3}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{|x+\frac{1}{3}|\sqrt{1+{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x+\frac{1}{3})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186539028
$\int \frac{4}{|x+1|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+2x+5}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2x+5 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}+5 \\  & =(x+1)^{2}+4\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{4}{|x+1|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+2x+5}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{4}{|x+1|\sqrt{4+(x+1)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{2}{|\frac{x+1}{2}|\sqrt{4+4{(\frac{x+1}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x+1}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x+1}{2})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=\frac{x+1}{2}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x+1}{2}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x+1}{2})}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =2\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-2\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-2\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(\frac{x+1}{2})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186563668
$\int \frac{\sqrt{2}}{|x-5|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-10x+27}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-10x+27 & ={x}^{2}-2\cdot 5x+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2}+27 \\  & =(x-5)^{2}+2\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}\int \frac{\sqrt{2}}{|x-5|\sqrt{{x}^{2}-10x+27}}\,\text{d}x & =\int \frac{\sqrt{2}}{|x-5|\sqrt{2+(x-5)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}}|\sqrt{2+2{(\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x \\  & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}})}^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.\end{aligned}
$$

Let $u=\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}}.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{2}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int \frac{1}{|\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}}|\sqrt{1+{(\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}})}^{2}}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\cdot \sqrt{2}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =-\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =-\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(\frac{x-5}{\sqrt{2}})+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186563699
$\int -\frac{15}{|5x+10|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+4x+5}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+4x+5 & ={x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}+5 \\  & =(x+2)^{2}+1\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int -\frac{15}{|5x-10|\sqrt{{x}^{2}+4x+5}}\,\text{d}x=-\int \frac{15}{5|x+2|\sqrt{1+(x+2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x=-3\int \frac{1}{|x+2|\sqrt{1+(x+2)^{2}}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=x+2.$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=1\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\begin{aligned}-3\int \frac{1}{|x+2|\sqrt{1+(x+2)^{2}}}\text{d}x & =-3\int \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1+{u}^{2}}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =3\operatorname{arcsch}⁡u+C \\  & =3\operatorname{arcsch}⁡(x+2)+C.\end{aligned}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186167437
Calculate $\int \frac{1}{9-(x+4)^{2}}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we note that

$$
\int \frac{1}{9-(x+4)^{2}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{9-9(\frac{1}{9}(x+4)^{2})}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{3}(x+4))}^{2}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{1}{3}(x+4).$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{3}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=3\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{3}(x+4))}^{2}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{9}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\cdot 3\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{3}{9}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{3}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x+4}{3})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{x+4}{3}|>1.$


QUESTION 17592186167438
$\int \frac{1}{1-4x-{x}^{2}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}1-4x-{x}^{2} & =-({x}^{2}+4x-1) \\  & =-({x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}-1) \\  & =-((x+2)^{2}-5) \\  & =5-(x+2)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{1-4x-{x}^{2}}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{5-(x+2)^{2}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{5}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(x+2))}^{2}}\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(x+2).$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{5}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{5}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(x+2))}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{5}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot \sqrt{5}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}(x+2))+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x+2}{\sqrt{5}})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{x+2}{\sqrt{5}}|>1.$


QUESTION 17592186538952
$\int \frac{1}{6x-{x}^{2}-5}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}6x-{x}^{2}-5 & =-({x}^{2}-6x+5) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}+5) \\  & =-((x-3)^{2}-4) \\  & =4-(x-3)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{6x-{x}^{2}-5}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{4-(x-3)^{2}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{2}(x-3))}^{2}}\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{1}{2}(x-3).$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{2}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=2\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{2}(x-3))}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot 2\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{1}{2}(x-3))+C \\  & =\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x-3}{2})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{x-3}{2}|>1.$


QUESTION 17592186538982
$\int \frac{1}{12+4x-{x}^{2}}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}12+4x-{x}^{2} & =-({x}^{2}-4x-12) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}-12) \\  & =-((x-2)^{2}-16) \\  & =16-(x-2)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{12+4x-{x}^{2}}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{16-(x-2)^{2}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{4}(x-2))}^{2}}\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{1}{4}(x-2).$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{4}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=4\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{4}(x-2))}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot 4\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{1}{4}(x-2))+C \\  & =\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x-2}{4})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{x-2}{4}|>1.$


QUESTION 17592186539017
$\int \frac{1}{12x-{x}^{2}-33}\text{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}12x-{x}^{2}-33 & =-({x}^{2}-12x+33) \\  & =-({x}^{2}-2\cdot 6x+{6}^{2}-{6}^{2}+33) \\  & =-((x-6)^{2}-3) \\  & =3-(x-6)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \frac{1}{12x-{x}^{2}-33}\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{3-(x-6)^{2}}\text{d}x=\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(x-6))}^{2}}\text{d}x.
$$

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\sqrt{3}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(x-6))}^{2}}\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{3}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\cdot \sqrt{3}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(x-6))+C \\  & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{3}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{x-6}{\sqrt{3}})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186563651
Calculate $\int \frac{1}{16-(3x+2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x=$

First, we note that

$$
\int \frac{1}{16-(3x+2)^{2}}\,\text{d}x=\int \frac{1}{16-16(\frac{1}{16}(3x+2)^{2})}\,\text{d}x=\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{4}(3x+2))}^{2}}\,\text{d}x.
$$

Let $u=\frac{1}{4}(3x+2).$ Then, we have

$$
\frac{\text{d}u}{\text{d}x}=\frac{3}{4}\,⟹\,\text{d}x=\frac{4}{3}\,\text{d}u.
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{(\frac{1}{4}(3x+2))}^{2}}\,\text{d}x & =\frac{1}{16}\int \frac{1}{1-{u}^{2}}\,\cdot \frac{4}{3}\,\text{d}u \\  & =\frac{1}{12}\cdot \operatorname{arcoth}⁡u+C \\  & =\frac{1}{12}\operatorname{arcoth}⁡(\frac{3x+2}{4})+C.\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition $|\frac{3x+2}{4}|>1.$


LESSON Solving Quadratic Equations by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186068431
Solve the equation $x^2 -10x = -24.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that $x^2 -10x +25$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
x^2 -10x +25 = (x-5)^2.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side: 

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-10x & =-24 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 5\cdot x & =-24 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 5\cdot x+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =-24 \\ (x-5{)}^{2}-25 & =-24\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding $25$ to both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-5{)}^{2}-25+25 & =-24+25 \\ (x-5{)}^{2} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-5{)}^{2} & =1 \\ \sqrt{(x-5{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{1} \\ |x-5| & =1 \\ x-5 & =\pm 1 \\ x & =5\pm 1\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =5-1=4 \\ {x}_{2} & =5+1=6\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186068498
Write the equation $x^2 + 6x +5= 0$ in the form $(x+p)^2=q,$ and hence solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+6x+5 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+6x & =-5 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 3\cdot x & =-5 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 3\cdot x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2} & =-5 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3\cdot x+{3}^{2})-9 & =-5 \\ (x+3{)}^{2}-9 & =-5\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+3{)}^{2}-9 & =-5 \\ (x+3{)}^{2} & =-5+9 \\ (x+3{)}^{2} & =4\end{aligned}
$$

 

So, $p=3$ and $q=4.$ Now, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+3{)}^{2} & =4 \\ \sqrt{(x+3{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{4} \\ |x+3| & =2 \\ x+3 & =\pm 2 \\ x & =-3\pm 2\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =-3+2=-1 \\ {x}_{2} & =-3-2=-5\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186068499
Solve the equation $x^2-2x-1=0.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-2x-1 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}-2x & =1 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x & =1 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =1 \\ ({x}^{2}-2x+1)-1 & =1 \\ (x-1{)}^{2}-1 & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square and solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1{)}^{2}-1 & =1 \\ (x-1{)}^{2} & =2 \\ \sqrt{(x-1{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{2} \\ |x-1| & =\sqrt{2} \\ x-1 & =\pm \sqrt{2} \\ x & =1\pm \sqrt{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, our solutions are

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =1+\sqrt{2}, \\ {x}_{2} & =1-\sqrt{2}.\end{aligned}
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186069947 Introduction
We can use the method of completing the square to solve *any* quadratic equation, even if the equation does not factor.

To illustrate, suppose we want to solve the following equation:

$$
x^2+2x-8=0
$$

We can do so by following the five steps below:

**Step 1.** Move the constant term to the right-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2x-8 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+2x & =8\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 2.** Complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2x & =8 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x & =8 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =8 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2})-1 & =8 \\ (x+1)^{2}-1 & =8\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 3.** Isolate the perfect square by adding $1$ to both sides. This gives

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+1)^{2} & =9.\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 4.** We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+1)^{2} & =9 \\ \sqrt{(x+1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{9} \\ |x+1| & =3 \\ x+1 & =\pm 3 \\ x & =-1\pm 3\end{aligned}
$$

**Step 5.** Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =-1-3=-4 \\ {x}_{2} & =-1+3=2\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $x=-4$ and $x=2.$


QUESTION 17592186069058
Let ${x}_{1}$ and ${x}_{2}$ be the solutions of the equation ${x}^{2}-2x=24.$ Find the value ${x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}^{2}.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}-2x+1$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-2x+1=(x-1{)}^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-2x & =24 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x & =24 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =24 \\ (x-1{)}^{2}-1 & =24\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding $1$ to both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1{)}^{2}-1+1 & =24+1 \\ (x-1{)}^{2} & =25\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1{)}^{2} & =25 \\ \sqrt{(x-1{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{25} \\ |x-1| & =5 \\ x-1 & =\pm 5 \\ x & =1\pm 5\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =1-5=-4 \\ {x}_{2} & =1+5=6\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
{x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}^{2}=(-4{)}^{2}+{6}^{2}=52.
$$

QUESTION 17592186272308
Let ${x}_{1}$ and ${x}_{2}$ be the solutions of the equation ${x}^{2}+4x=12.$ Find the value ${x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}^{2}.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}+4x+4$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}+4x+4=(x+2{)}^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+4x & =12 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 2\cdot x & =12 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 2\cdot x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2} & =12 \\ (x+2{)}^{2}-4 & =12\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding $4$ to both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+2{)}^{2}-4+4 & =12+4 \\ (x+2{)}^{2} & =16\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+2{)}^{2} & =16 \\ \sqrt{(x+2{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{16} \\ |x+2| & =4 \\ x+2 & =\pm 4 \\ x & =-2\pm 4\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =-2-4=-6 \\ {x}_{2} & =-2+4=2\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
{x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}^{2}=(-6{)}^{2}+{2}^{2}=40.
$$

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}-8x+16$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-8x+16=(x-4{)}^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-8x & =20 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 4\cdot x & =20 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =20 \\ (x-4{)}^{2}-16 & =20\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding $16$ to both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-4{)}^{2}-16+16 & =20+16 \\ (x-4{)}^{2} & =36\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-4{)}^{2} & =36 \\ \sqrt{(x-4{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{36} \\ |x-4| & =6 \\ x-4 & =\pm 6 \\ x & =4\pm 6\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =4-6=-2 \\ {x}_{2} & =4+6=10\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
{x}_{1}^{2}+{x}_{2}^{2}=(-2{)}^{2}+{10}^{2}=104.
$$

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-10x+25=(x-5{)}^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-10x & =-21 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 5\cdot x & =-21 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 5\cdot x+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =-21 \\ (x-5{)}^{2}-25 & =-21\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding $25$ to both sides: 

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-5{)}^{2}-25+25 & =-21+25 \\ (x-5{)}^{2} & =4\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186069217
The equation ${x}^{2}-4x+4=9$ can be written in the form $(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $\,p+q,$ and the sum of the roots ${x}_{1}+{x}_{2}.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-4x+4 & =9 \\ {x}^{2}-4x & =5 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 2\cdot x & =5 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot 2\cdot x+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2} & =5 \\ ({x}^{2}-2\cdot 2\cdot x+{2}^{2})-4 & =5 \\ (x-2)^{2}-4 & =5\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-2)^{2}-4 & =5 \\ (x-2)^{2} & =9\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=-2,q=9,$ and $p+q=7.$

Now, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-2)^{2} & =9 \\ \sqrt{(x-2)^{2}} & =\sqrt{9} \\ x-2 & =\pm 3 \\ x & =2\pm 3\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =2+3=5 \\ {x}_{2} & =2-3=-1\end{aligned}
$$

So, the solutions are ${x}_{1}=-1$ and ${x}_{2}=5.$ Thus, the sum is $4.$


QUESTION 17592186069267
The equation ${x}^{2}+8x-33=0$ can be written in the form $(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $\,p+q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+8x-33 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+8x & =33 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x & =33 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =33 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2})-16 & =33 \\ (x+4)^{2}-16 & =33\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square and solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+4)^{2}-16 & =33 \\ (x+4)^{2} & =49\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=4,q=49,$ and $p+q=53.$

Next, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+4)^{2} & =49 \\ \sqrt{(x+4)^{2}} & =\sqrt{49} \\ x+4 & =\pm 7 \\ x & =-4\pm 7\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}x & =-4+7=3 \\ x & =-4-7=-11\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $x=-11$ and $x=3.$

QUESTION 17592186317325

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{a}^{2}+8a & =9 \\ {a}^{2}+2\cdot a\cdot 4 & =9 \\ {a}^{2}+2\cdot a\cdot 4+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =9 \\ ({a}^{2}+2\cdot a\cdot 4+{4}^{2})-{4}^{2} & =9 \\ ({a}^{2}+2\cdot a\cdot 4+{4}^{2})-16 & =9 \\ (a+4{)}^{2}-16 & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(a+4{)}^{2}-16 & =9 \\ (a+4{)}^{2} & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$

$$

$$

Therefore, the solutions are $a=1$ and $a=-9.$

QUESTION 17592186399739
The equation ${t}^{2}-14t+5=5$ can be written in the form $(t+p{)}^{2}=q.$ Find $\,pq\,,$ and the sum of the roots ${t}_{1}+{t}_{2}.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{t}^{2}-14t+5 & =5 \\ {t}^{2}-14t & =0 \\ {t}^{2}-2\cdot t\cdot 7+{7}^{2}-{7}^{2} & =0 \\ (t-7{)}^{2}-{7}^{2} & =0 \\ (t-7{)}^{2} & =49\end{aligned}
$$

Hence, $p=-7$ and $q=49.$ Thus, $pq=-343.$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(t-7{)}^{2} & =49 \\ \sqrt{(t-7{)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{49} \\ |t-7| & =7 \\ t-7 & =\pm 7 \\ t & =7\pm 7\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{t}_{1} & =7-7=0 \\ {t}_{2} & =7+7=14\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the sum is ${t}_{1}+{t}_{2}=14.$


QUESTION 17592186401961
The equation ${x}^{2}-2x+1=1$ can be written in the form $(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $\,pq\,,$ and the sum of the roots ${x}_{1}+{x}_{2}.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}-2x+1 & =1 \\ {x}^{2}-2x & =0 \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot x\cdot 1+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =0 \\ (x-1)^{2}-{1}^{2} & =0 \\ (x-1)^{2} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Hence, $p=-1$ and $q=1.$ Thus, $pq=-1.$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2} & =1 \\ \sqrt{(x-1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{1} \\ |x-1| & =1 \\ x-1 & =\pm 1 \\ x & =1\pm 1\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =1-1=0 \\ {x}_{2} & =1+1=2\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the sum is ${x}_{1}+{x}_{2}=2.$

$$
\begin{aligned}{t}^{2}+10t+13 & =0 \\ {t}^{2}+10t & =-13 \\ {t}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot t & =-13 \\ {t}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot t+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =-13 \\ (t+5)^{2}-25 & =-13\end{aligned}

$$
\begin{aligned}{z}^{2}-50 & =-10z \\ {z}^{2}+10z & =50 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot z & =50 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot z+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =50 \\ (z+5{)}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =50\end{aligned}
$$

$$

QUESTION 17592186265778
The equation ${a}^{2}+8a=18$ can be written in the form $(a+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{a}^{2}+8a & =18 \\ {a}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot a & =18 \\ {a}^{2}+2\cdot a\cdot 4+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =18 \\ (a+4)^{2}-{4}^{2} & =18\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(a+4)^{2}-{4}^{2} & =18 \\ (a+4)^{2}-16 & =18 \\ (a+4)^{2} & =34\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=4,q=34,$ and $p+q=38.$

Next, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(a+4)^{2} & =34 \\ \sqrt{(a+4)^{2}} & =\sqrt{34} \\ |a+4| & =\sqrt{34} \\ a+4 & =\pm \sqrt{34} \\ a & =-4\pm \sqrt{34}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186265789
The equation ${z}^{2}-25=-10z$ can be written in the form $(z+p{)}^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, the $-10z$ term to the left-hand side, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}{z}^{2}-25 & =-10z \\ {z}^{2}+10z & =25 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot z & =25 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 5\cdot z+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =25 \\ (z+5{)}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(z+5{)}^{2}-{5}^{2} & =25 \\ (z+5{)}^{2} & =50\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=5,q=50,$ and $p+q=55.$


QUESTION 17592186290360
The equation ${x}^{2}+8x+9=0$ is equivalent to$\,(x\,\,+$
{{field-1}}
$)^{2}=$
{{field-2}}, and the solutions are 

$x=$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+8x+9 & =0 \\ {x}^{2}+8x & =-9 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x & =-9 \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =-9 \\ ({x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2})-16 & =-9 \\ (x+4)^{2}-16 & =-9\end{aligned}

QUESTION 17592186354468
The equation ${y}^{2}+16=-12y$ can be written in the form $(y+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, the $-12y$ term to the left-hand side, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}{y}^{2}+16 & =-12y \\ {y}^{2}+12y & =-16 \\ {y}^{2}+2\cdot 6\cdot y & =-16 \\ {y}^{2}+2\cdot 6\cdot y+{6}^{2}-{6}^{2} & =-16 \\ (y+6)^{2}-{6}^{2} & =-16\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186399746
The equation ${m}^{2}+8=-8m$ can be written in the form $(m+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, the $-8m$ term to the left-hand side, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}{m}^{2}+8 & =-8m \\ {m}^{2}+8m & =-8 \\ {m}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot m & =-8 \\ {m}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot m+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2} & =-8 \\ (m+4)^{2}-{4}^{2} & =-8\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}(m+4)^{2}-{4}^{2} & =-8 \\ (m+4)^{2} & =8\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=4,q=8,$ and $p+q=12.$

Next, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(m+4)^{2} & =8 \\ \sqrt{(m+4)^{2}} & =\sqrt{8} \\ |m+4| & =\sqrt{8} \\ m+4 & =\pm 2\sqrt{2} \\ m & =-4\pm 2\sqrt{2}\end{aligned}
$$


LESSON Finding the Center and Axes of Ellipses by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186087527
Find the center and the lengths of the major and minor axes of the ellipse whose equation is $25x^2 + 9y^2 - 100x - 18y = 116.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}25{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}-100x-18y & =116 \\ (25{x}^{2}-100x)+(9{y}^{2}-18y) & =116\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for the both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(25{x}^{2}-100x)+(9{y}^{2}-18y) & =116 \\ 25[{x}^{2}-4x]+9[{y}^{2}-2y] & =116 \\ 25[(x-2)^{2}-4]+9[(y-1)^{2}-1] & =116 \\ 25(x-2)^{2}-100+9(y-1)^{2}-9 & =116\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}25(x-2)^{2}-100+9(y-1)^{2}-9 & =116 \\ 25(x-2)^{2}+9(y-1)^{2} & =116+100+9 \\ 25(x-2)^{2}+9(y-1)^{2} & =225 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{9}+\frac{(y-1)^{2}}{25} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{{3}^{2}}+\frac{(y-1)^{2}}{{5}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $5 > 3,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 5 =10,$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 3 =6,$ and the ellipse is centered at $({2},{1}).$


EXAMPLE 17592186087528
Find the center and the lengths of the major and minor axes of the ellipse whose equation is $x^2 + 4y^2 - 6x + 24y + 41 = 0.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}-6x+24y+41 & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}-6x)+(4{y}^{2}+24y)+41 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for the both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}-6x)+(4{y}^{2}+24y)+41 & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}-6x)+4[{y}^{2}+6y]+41 & =0 \\ (x-3)^{2}-9+4[(y+3)^{2}-9]+41 & =0 \\ (x-3)^{2}-9+4(y+3)^{2}-36+41 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-3)^{2}-9+4(y+3)^{2}-36+41 & =0 \\ (x-3)^{2}+4(y+3)^{2} & =9+36-41 \\ (x-3)^{2}+4(y+3)^{2} & =4 \\ \frac{(x-3)^{2}}{4}+\frac{(y+3)^{2}}{1} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-3)^{2}}{{2}^{2}}+\frac{(y+3)^{2}}{{1}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $2 > 1,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 2 =4,$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 1 =2,$ and the ellipse is centered at $({3},{-3}).$


TUTORIAL 17592186088911 Introduction
Let's consider the equation $4x^2+y^2 - 8x - 4y = 8.$ Its graph is given below.

![](images/bb/bb75dab958d3dedba6e6b685b88f393aa6c28c15c2e52b414a30e97fdfa68a9e.png)

Clearly, this is an ellipse. To determine the center and the lengths of the major and minor axes, we need to rewrite the given equation in the standard form

$$
\dfrac{(x-h)^2}{{a}^2}+\dfrac{(y-k)^2}{{b}^2}=1,
$$

where $(h,k)$ is the center of the ellipse, and $a$ and $b$ are the horizontal and vertical radii of the ellipse, respectively. 

We do this by completing the square. First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-8x-4y & =8 \\ (4{x}^{2}-8x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =8\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for the both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(4{x}^{2}-8x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =8 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-2x]+{y}^{2}-4y & =8 \\ 4[(x-1)^{2}-1]+(y-2)^{2}-4 & =8 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}-4+(y-2)^{2}-4 & =8.\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-1)^{2}-4+(y-2)^{2}-4 & =8 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & =8+4+4 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & =16 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{4}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{16} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{{2}^{2}}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{{4}^{2}} & =1.\end{aligned}
$$

Now that the ellipse equation is in the standard form, we can tell that the ellipse is centered at $(1,2).$

Also, since $4 > 2,$ the length of the semi-major axis is $4$, and the length of the semi-minor axis is $2.$

Consequently, the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 4 =8$ and the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 2 =4.$

QUESTION 17592186250395
Determine the center of the ellipse whose equation is $3{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+36x+32y=-124.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+36x+32y & =-124 \\ (3{x}^{2}+36x)+(4{y}^{2}+32y) & =-124\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(3{x}^{2}+36x)+(4{y}^{2}+32y) & =-124 \\ 3[{x}^{2}+12x]+4[{y}^{2}+8y] & =-124 \\ 3[(x+6)^{2}-{6}^{2}]+4[(y+4)^{2}-{4}^{2}] & =-124 \\ 3[(x+6)^{2}-36]+4[(y+4)^{2}-16] & =-124 \\ 3(x+6)^{2}-108+4(y+4)^{2}-64 & =-124\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}3(x+6)^{2}-108+4(y+4)^{2}-64 & =-124 \\ 3(x+6)^{2}+4(y+4)^{2} & =-124+108+64 \\ 3(x+6)^{2}+4(y+4)^{2} & =48 \\ \frac{(x+6)^{2}}{16}+\frac{(y+4)^{2}}{12} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+6)^{2}}{{4}^{2}}+\frac{(y+4)^{2}}{(2\sqrt{3})^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(-6,-4).$


QUESTION 17592186295105
Determine the lengths of the major and minor axes of the ellipse whose equation is $2{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+12x-4y=-20.$

$\,$ The length of the major axis is 
{{field-1}}.

$\,$ The length of the minor axis is
{{field-2}}.

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+12x-4y & =-20 \\ (2{x}^{2}+12x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =-20\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(2{x}^{2}+12x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =-20 \\ 2({x}^{2}+6x)+({y}^{2}-4y) & =-20 \\ 2[(x+3)^{2}-9]+[(y-2)^{2}-4] & =-20 \\ 2(x+3)^{2}-18+(y-2)^{2}-4 & =-20\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2(x+3)^{2}-18+(y-2)^{2}-4 & =-20 \\ 2(x+3)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & =-20+18+4 \\ 2(x+3)^{2}+(y-2)^{2} & =2 \\ \frac{(x+3)^{2}}{1}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{2} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+3)^{2}}{{1}^{2}}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{(\sqrt{2})^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $\sqrt{2}>1,$ the length of the major axis  $=2\cdot \sqrt{2}=2\sqrt{2},$ and the length of the minor axis  $=2\cdot 1=2.$


QUESTION 17592186295106
Determine the lengths of the major and minor axes of the ellipse whose equation is $4{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}-16x+18y=119.$

$\,$ The length of the major axis is 
{{field-1}}.

$\,$ The length of the minor axis is
{{field-2}}.

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}-16x+18y & =119 \\ (4{x}^{2}-16x)+(9{y}^{2}+18y) & =119\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(4{x}^{2}-16x)+(9{y}^{2}+18y) & =119 \\ 4({x}^{2}-4x)+9({y}^{2}+2y) & =119 \\ 4[(x-2)^{2}-4]+9[(y+1)^{2}-1] & =119 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}-16+9(y+1)^{2}-9 & =119\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-2)^{2}-16+9(y+1)^{2}-9 & =119 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}+9(y+1)^{2} & =119+16+9 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}+9(y+1)^{2} & =144 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{36}+\frac{(y+1)^{2}}{16} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{{6}^{2}}+\frac{(y+1)^{2}}{{4}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $6>4,$ the length of the major axis  $=2\cdot 6=12,$ and the length of the minor axis  $=2\cdot 4=8.$


QUESTION 17592186341888
Determine the center of the ellipse whose equation is $5{x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}+20x-12y=-28.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}+20x-12y & =-28 \\ (5{x}^{2}+20x)+(2{y}^{2}-12y) & =-28\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(5{x}^{2}+20x)+(2{y}^{2}-12y) & =-28 \\ 5({x}^{2}+4x)+2({y}^{2}-6y) & =-28 \\ 5[(x+2)^{2}-4]+2[(y-3)^{2}-9] & =-28 \\ 5(x+2)^{2}-20+2(y-3)^{2}-18 & =-28\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}5(x+2)^{2}-20+2(y-3)^{2}-18 & =-28 \\ 5(x+2)^{2}+2(y-3)^{2} & =-28+20+18 \\ 5(x+2)^{2}+2(y-3)^{2} & =10 \\ \frac{(x+2)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(y-3)^{2}}{5} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(-2,3).$


QUESTION 17592186345205
Determine the center of the ellipse whose equation is $5{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}-10x-36y=4.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}-10x-36y & =4 \\ (5{x}^{2}-10x)+(9{y}^{2}-36y) & =4\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(5{x}^{2}-10x)+(9{y}^{2}-36y) & =4 \\ 5[{x}^{2}-2x]+9[{y}^{2}-4y] & =4 \\ 5[(x-1)^{2}-{1}^{2}]+9[(y-2)^{2}-{2}^{2}] & =4 \\ 5[(x-1)^{2}-1]+9[(y-2)^{2}-4] & =4 \\ 5(x-1)^{2}-5+9(y-2)^{2}-36 & =4\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}5(x-1)^{2}-5+9(y-2)^{2}-36 & =4 \\ 5(x-1)^{2}+9(y-2)^{2} & =4+5+36 \\ 5(x-1)^{2}+9(y-2)^{2} & =45 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{9}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{5} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{{3}^{2}}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{(\sqrt{5})^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(1,2).$


QUESTION 17592186396134
Consider the ellipse whose equation is ${x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}-2x+12y=-11.$ Which of the following statements are true?

Select all that apply.

- **I.** Its center is $(1,-3)$
- **II.** The length of the major axis  is $2\sqrt{2}$
- **III.** The length of the minor axis is $4$

First, we must group the $x$ and $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2{y}^{2}-2x+12y & =-11 \\ ({x}^{2}-2x)+(2{y}^{2}+12y) & =-11\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}-2x)+(2{y}^{2}+12y) & =-11 \\ [{x}^{2}-2x]+2[{y}^{2}+6y] & =-11 \\ [(x-1)^{2}-{1}^{2}]+2[(y+3)^{2}-{3}^{2}] & =-11 \\ [(x-1)^{2}-1]+2[(y+3)^{2}-9] & =-11 \\ (x-1)^{2}-1+2(y+3)^{2}-18 & =-11\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2}-1+2(y+3)^{2}-18 & =-11 \\ (x-1)^{2}+2(y+3)^{2} & =-11+1+18 \\ (x-1)^{2}+2(y+3)^{2} & =8 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{8}+\frac{(y+3)^{2}}{4} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-1)^{2}}{(2\sqrt{2})^{2}}+\frac{(y+3)^{2}}{{2}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $2\sqrt{2}>2,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 2\sqrt{2}=4\sqrt{2},$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 2=4,$ and the ellipse is centered at $(1,-3).$

Therefore,  I and III are true, and II is false.


QUESTION 17592186403606
Determine  the lengths of the major and minor axes of the ellipse whose equation is ${x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}-4x+16y=-16.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}-4x+16y & =-16 \\ ({x}^{2}-4x)+(4{y}^{2}+16y) & =-16\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we must complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}-4x)+(4{y}^{2}+16y) & =-16 \\ ({x}^{2}-4x)+4({y}^{2}+4y) & =-16 \\ [(x-2)^{2}-4]+4[(y+2)^{2}-4] & =-16 \\ (x-2)^{2}-4+4(y+2)^{2}-16 & =-16\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-2)^{2}-4+4(y+2)^{2}-16 & =-16 \\ (x-2)^{2}+4(y+2)^{2} & =-16+16+4 \\ (x-2)^{2}+4(y+2)^{2} & =4 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{4}+\frac{(y+2)^{2}}{1} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{{2}^{2}}+\frac{(y+2)^{2}}{{1}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $2>1,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 2=4,$ and the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 1=2.$

QUESTION 17592186250396
Determine the center of the ellipse whose equation is
$2{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+16x-2y+29=0.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}+16x-2y+29 & =0 \\ (2{x}^{2}+16x)+({y}^{2}-2y)+29 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(2{x}^{2}+16x)+({y}^{2}-2y)+29 & =0 \\ 2[{x}^{2}+8x]+[{y}^{2}-2y]+29 & =0 \\ 2[(x+4)^{2}-{4}^{2}]+[(y-1)^{2}-{1}^{2}]+29 & =0 \\ 2[(x+4)^{2}-16]+[(y-1)^{2}-1]+29 & =0 \\ 2(x+4)^{2}-32+(y-1)^{2}-1+29 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2(x+4)^{2}-32+(y-1)^{2}-1+29 & =0 \\ 2(x+4)^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & =32+1-29 \\ 2(x+4)^{2}+(y-1)^{2} & =4 \\ \frac{(x+4)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(y-1)^{2}}{4} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+4)^{2}}{(\sqrt{2})^{2}}+\frac{(y-1)^{2}}{{2}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(-4,1).$


QUESTION 17592186284557
Determine the length of the minor axis of the ellipse whose equation is  $25{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+100x–40y+100=0.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}25{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+100x–40y+100 & =0 \\ (25{x}^{2}+100x)+(4{y}^{2}-40y)+100 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(25{x}^{2}+100x)+(4{y}^{2}-40y)+100 & =0 \\ 25[{x}^{2}+4x]+4[{y}^{2}-10y]+100 & =0 \\ 25[(x+2)^{2}-{2}^{2}]+4[(y-5)^{2}-{5}^{2}]+100 & =0 \\ 25[(x+2)^{2}-4]+4[(y-5)^{2}-25]+100 & =0 \\ 25(x+2)^{2}-100+4(y-5)^{2}-100+100 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}25(x+2)^{2}-100+4(y-5)^{2}-100+100 & =0 \\ 25(x+2)^{2}+4(y-5)^{2} & =-100+100+100 \\ 25(x+2)^{2}+4(y-5)^{2} & =100 \\ \frac{(x+2)^{2}}{4}+\frac{(y-5)^{2}}{25} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+2)^{2}}{{2}^{2}}+\frac{(y-5)^{2}}{{5}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $5>2,$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 2=4.$


QUESTION 17592186284558
Consider the ellipse whose equation is $4{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}–48x+72y+144=0.$ Which of the following statements are true?

Select all that apply.

- **I.** Its center is $(6,-4)$
- **II.** The length of the major axis is $6$
- **III.** The length of the minor axis is $4$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+9{y}^{2}–48x+72y+144 & =0 \\ (4{x}^{2}-48x)+(9{y}^{2}+72y)+144 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(4{x}^{2}-48x)+(9{y}^{2}+72y)+144 & =0 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-12x]+9[{y}^{2}+8y]+144 & =0 \\ 4[(x-6)^{2}-{6}^{2}]+9[(y+4)^{2}-{4}^{2}]+144 & =0 \\ 4[(x-6)^{2}-36]+9[(y+4)^{2}-16]+144 & =0 \\ 4(x-6)^{2}-144+9(y+4)^{2}-144+144 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-6)^{2}-144+9(y+4)^{2}-144+144 & =0 \\ 4(x-6)^{2}+9(y+4)^{2} & =-144+144+144 \\ 4(x-6)^{2}+9(y+4)^{2} & =144 \\ \frac{(x-6)^{2}}{36}+\frac{(y+4)^{2}}{16} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-6)^{2}}{{6}^{2}}+\frac{(y+4)^{2}}{{4}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $6>4,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 6=12,$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot 4=8,$ and the ellipse is centered at $(6,-4).$

Therefore, statement I is true, statements II and III are false.


QUESTION 17592186284559
Determine the length of the major axis of the ellipse whose equation is ${x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+10x-16y+25=0.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+4{y}^{2}+10x-16y+25 & =0 \\ ({x}^{2}+10x)+(4{y}^{2}-16y)+25 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}+10x)+(4{y}^{2}-16y)+25 & =0 \\ [{x}^{2}+10x]+4[{y}^{2}-4y]+25 & =0 \\ [(x+5)^{2}-{5}^{2}]+4[(y-2)^{2}-{2}^{2}]+25 & =0 \\ [(x+5)^{2}-25]+4[(y-2)^{2}-4]+25 & =0 \\ (x+5)^{2}-25+4(y-2)^{2}-16+25 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+5)^{2}-25+4(y-2)^{2}-16+25 & =0 \\ (x+5)^{2}+4(y-2)^{2} & =25-25+16 \\ (x+5)^{2}+4(y-2)^{2} & =16 \\ \frac{(x+5)^{2}}{16}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{4} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+5)^{2}}{{4}^{2}}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{{2}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $4>2,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 4=8.$


QUESTION 17592186284560
Determine the center of the ellipse whose equation is
$4{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-16x+2y+13=0.$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-16x+2y+13 & =0 \\ (4{x}^{2}-16x)+({y}^{2}+2y)+13 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(4{x}^{2}-16x)+({y}^{2}+2y)+13 & =0 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-4x]+[{y}^{2}+2y]+13 & =0 \\ 4[(x-2)^{2}-{2}^{2}]+[(y+1)^{2}-{(1)}^{2}]+13 & =0 \\ 4[(x-2)^{2}-4]+[(y+1)^{2}-1]+13 & =0 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}-16+(y+1)^{2}-1+13 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-2)^{2}-16+(y+1)^{2}-1+13 & =0 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}+(y+1)^{2} & =-13+1+16 \\ 4(x-2)^{2}+(y+1)^{2} & =4 \\ (x-2)^{2}+\frac{(y+1)^{2}}{4} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-2)^{2}}{{1}^{2}}+\frac{(y+1)^{2}}{{2}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(2,-1).$


QUESTION 17592186284566
Consider the ellipse whose equation is $3{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-9=0.$ Which of the following statements are true?

Select all that apply.

- **I.** Its center is $(0,0)$
- **II.** The length of the major axis is $3$
- **III.** The length of the minor axis is $2\sqrt{3}$

We rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}+{y}^{2}-9 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}+{y}^{2} & =9 \\ \frac{{x}^{2}}{3}+\frac{{y}^{2}}{9} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-0)^{2}}{(\sqrt{3})^{2}}+\frac{(y-0)^{2}}{{3}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $3>\sqrt{3},$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 3=6,$ the length of the minor axis is $2\cdot \sqrt{3}=2\sqrt{3},$ and the ellipse is centered at $(0,0).$

Therefore, statements I and III are true, statement II is false.


QUESTION 17592186295107
The center of the ellipse $6{x}^{2}+5{y}^{2}-36x+50y-1=0$ is located at the point$\,($
{{field-1}},
{{field-2}}$).$

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}6{x}^{2}+5{y}^{2}-36x+50y-1 & =0 \\ (6{x}^{2}-36x)+(5{y}^{2}+50y)-1 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(6{x}^{2}-36x)+(5{y}^{2}+50y)-1 & =0 \\ 6[{x}^{2}-6x]+5[{y}^{2}+10y]-1 & =0 \\ 6[(x-3)^{2}-{3}^{2}]+5[(y+5)^{2}-{5}^{2}]-1 & =0 \\ 6[(x-3)^{2}-9]+5[(y+5)^{2}-25]-1 & =0 \\ 6(x-3)^{2}-54+5(y+5)^{2}-125-1 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}6(x-3)^{2}-54+5(y+5)^{2}-125-1 & =0 \\ 6(x-3)^{2}+5(y+5)^{2} & =1+54+125 \\ 6(x-3)^{2}+5(y+5)^{2} & =180 \\ \frac{(x-3)^{2}}{30}+\frac{(y+5)^{2}}{36} & =1 \\ \frac{(x-3)^{2}}{(\sqrt{30})^{2}}+\frac{(y+5)^{2}}{{6}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the ellipse is centered at $(3,-5).$


QUESTION 17592186295108
For the ellipse $9{x}^{2}+25{y}^{2}+72x-100y+19=0$, the length of the major axis is{{field-1}}.

First, we must group the $x$ terms and the $y$ terms:

$$
\begin{aligned}9{x}^{2}+25{y}^{2}+72x-100y+19 & =0 \\ (9{x}^{2}+72x)+(25{y}^{2}-100y)+19 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we complete the square for both the $x$ and $y$ terms, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}(9{x}^{2}+72x)+(25{y}^{2}-100y)+19 & =0 \\ 9[{x}^{2}+8x]+25[{y}^{2}-4y]+19 & =0 \\ 9[(x+4)^{2}-{4}^{2}]+25[(y-2)^{2}-{2}^{2}]+19 & =0 \\ 9[(x+4)^{2}-16]+25[(y-2)^{2}-4]+19 & =0 \\ 9(x+4)^{2}-144+25(y-2)^{2}-100+19 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we rearrange the equation into the desired form:

$$
\begin{aligned}9(x+4)^{2}-144+25(y-2)^{2}-100+19 & =0 \\ 9(x+4)^{2}+25(y-2)^{2} & =-19+100+144 \\ 9(x+4)^{2}+25(y-2)^{2} & =225 \\ \frac{(x+4)^{2}}{25}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{9} & =1 \\ \frac{(x+4)^{2}}{{5}^{2}}+\frac{(y-2)^{2}}{{3}^{2}} & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Since $5>3,$ the length of the major axis is $2\cdot 5=10.$


LESSON Integration Using Hyperbolic Functions and Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186166618
${\displaystyle \int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt{x^2-2x+2}} =}$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned} x^2-2x+2 & = x^2 - 2\cdot x +1^2 - 1^2 + 2 \\[3pt] &= (x-1)^2 -1+2 \\[3pt] &= (x-1)^2 + 1 \end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt{x^2-2x+2}} = \int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt {(x-1)^2 + 1}}
$$

Let $u = x-1$. Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 2x + 2}} &= \int(dx)/(\sqrt{(x - 1)^{2} + 1}) \\
&= \int\frac{du}{\sqrt{u^{2} + 1}} \\
&= arsinh u + C \\
&= arsinh (x - 1) + C
\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186166619
${\displaystyle \int \dfrac{2}{\sqrt{x^2-4x+3}} \textrm{d}x=}$

First, we complete the square for the argument of the square root in the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^{2} - 4x + 3 &= x^{2} - 2 \cdot 2x + 2^{2} - 2^{2} + 3 \\
&= (x - 2)^{2} - 1
\end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the given integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{2}{\sqrt{x^2-4x+3}} \textrm{d}x = \int \dfrac{2}{\sqrt {(x-2)^2-1}} \, \textrm{d}x
$$

Let $u = x-2$. Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=1 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \textrm{d}x = \textrm{d}u
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int\frac{2}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 4x + 3}}dx &= \int(2)/(\sqrt{(x - 2)^{2} - 1})dx \\
&= 2\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{u^{2} - 1}}du \\
&= 2arcosh u + C \\
&= 2arcosh (x - 2) + C
\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186166620
$\displaystyle \int \dfrac{1}{4x-x^2} \textrm{d}x=$

First, we complete the square for the denominator:

$$
\begin{aligned} 4x-x^2 & = -(x^2 - 4x) \\[5pt] & = -(x^2 - 2\cdot 2x + 2^2-2^2) \\[5pt] & = -((x-2)^2 - 4) \\[5pt] &= 4 - (x-2)^2 \end{aligned}
$$

Substituting the above into the denominator of our integral, we get

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{4x-x^2}\textrm{d}x = \int \dfrac{1}{4-(x-2)^2}\textrm{d}x = \dfrac{1}{4} {\int}\dfrac{1}{1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}(x-2)\right)^2}\textrm{d}x
$$

Let

$$
u = \dfrac{1}{2}(x-2)
$$

Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{2} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \textrm{d}x = 2\,\textrm{d}u
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{4}\int(1)/(1 - (\frac{1}{2}(x - 2))^{2})dx &= \frac{1}{4}\int\frac{1}{1 - u^{2}} \cdot 2du \\
&= \frac{1}{2}\int\frac{1}{1 - u^{2}}du \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \cdot artanh u + C \\
&= \frac{1}{2}artanh (\frac{x - 2}{2}) + C
\end{aligned}
$$

Note that this result is subject to the condition

$$
\left|\dfrac {x-2} {2} \right| < 1
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186166621
Calculate $\displaystyle \int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt{\left(x - 1\right)^2-9}}$.

First, we note that

$$
\int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt{\left(x - 1\right)^2-9}} = \int \dfrac{\textrm{d}x}{\sqrt{9\left(\dfrac{x - 1}{3}\right)^2-9}} = \dfrac{1}{3} \int \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{\left(\dfrac{x-1}{3}\right)^2-1}} \textrm{d}x
$$

Let

$$
u =\dfrac{x-1}{3}
$$

Then, we have

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{3} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \textrm{d}x = 3\,\textrm{d}u
$$

Therefore,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{3}\int(1)/(\sqrt{(\frac{x - 1}{3})^{2} - 1})dx &= \frac{1}{3}\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{u^{2} - 1}} \cdot 3du \\
&= arcosh u + C \\
&= arcosh (\frac{x - 1}{3}) + C
\end{aligned}
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186168098 Introduction
Consider the integral

$$
\int \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x^2-10x+34}}\, \textrm{d}x
$$

As it is, this integral doesn't resemble any of the basic integrals we have encountered so far. However, we can complete the square for the denominator, as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}
x^{2} - 10x + 34 &= x^{2} - 10x + (25 - 25) + 34 \\
&= (x^{2} - 10x + 25) - 25 + 34 \\
&= (x - 5)^{2} + 9
\end{aligned}
$$

If we plug the above into our integral and collect a factor of $9$ in the denominator, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 10x + 34}}dx &= \int(1)/(\sqrt{(x - 5)^{2} + 9})dx \\
&= \int(1)/(\sqrt{9[((x - 5)^{2})/(9) + 1]})dx \\
&= \frac{1}{3}\int(1)/(\sqrt{(\frac{x - 5}{3})^{2} + 1})dx
\end{aligned}
$$

This now looks more familiar! Let

$$
u = \dfrac {x-5}{3}
$$

Then

$$
\dfrac{\textrm{d}u}{\textrm{d}x}=\dfrac{1}{3} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \textrm{d}x = 3\,\textrm{d}u
$$

We can now write the integral as

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{x^{2} - 10x + 34}}dx &= \frac{1}{3}\int(1)/(\sqrt{(\frac{x - 5}{3})^{2} + 1})dx \\
&= \frac{1}{3}\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{u^{2} + 1}} \cdot (3du) \\
&= \int\frac{1}{\sqrt{u^{2} + 1}}du
\end{aligned}
$$

This is a basic integral for an inverse hyperbolic function, and we can solve it in one step:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\int\frac{1}{\sqrt{u^{2} + 1}}du &= arsinh u + C \\
&= arsinh (\frac{x - 5}{3}) + C
\end{aligned}
$$

LESSON Completing the Square With Odd Linear Terms


EXAMPLE 17592186183961
Rewrite the expression 

$$
\left(x^2 + 7x + \dfrac{49}{4}\right) + 3
$$

as the sum of a perfect square and a constant.

First, we recall the following formula:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}{\color{blue}{a}}x + a^2 = (x + {\color{blue}{a}})^2
$$

Notice that we have a perfect square in the parentheses that we can factor as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+7x+\frac{49}{4} & = \\ {x}^{2}+7x+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2} & = \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{7}{2}x+{(\frac{7}{2})}^{2} & = \\ {(x+\frac{7}{2})}^{2} & \end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, we obtain

$$
\begin{aligned}({x}^{2}+7x+\frac{49}{4})+3 & = \\ {(x+\frac{7}{2})}^{2}+3 & .\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186183962
Complete the square on the quadratic expression $x^2 + 7x - 1.$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}x - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

Now, here's the trick. We *add* and *then subtract* $\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}{-1}}$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}x  + \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2} - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2 - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
\left(x + {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2  - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2 - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

Finally, evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
\left(x + {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2  - \dfrac{53}{4}.
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186183963
Complete the square on the quadratic expression $x^2 - 7x.$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 - {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}x
$$

Now, here's the trick. We *add* and *then subtract* $\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2{:}$ 

$$
\underbrace{x^2 - {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}x + \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2} - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
\left(x - {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2  - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2
$$

Finally, evaluating the last term, we get

$$
\left(x - {\color{blue}{\dfrac{7}{2}}}\right)^2  - \dfrac{49}{4}.
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186185383 Introduction
Let's recall the formula for factoring a perfect square:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}{\color{blue}{a}}x + a^2 = (x + {\color{blue}{a}})^2
$$

As we've seen, **completing the square** on a quadratic expression means expressing it as the sum of a perfect square and a constant.

In this lesson, we'll learn how to complete the square on a quadratic expression when the coefficient of the linear term is odd. In these cases, we get a fraction in the final expression.

As an example, let's consider the following quadratic expression:

$$
\left(x^2 + 5x + \left(\dfrac 52\right)^2\right) + 1
$$

Notice that we have a perfect square in the large parentheses that we can factor as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+5x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & = \\ {x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & = \\ {(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & \end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, our original expression can be written as

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}+1.\end{aligned}
$$

Let's see another example.


TUTORIAL 17592186185384 Completing the Square
Let's complete the square on the following expression: 

$$
x^2 + 3x + 4
$$

The procedure is very similar to the one we encountered when the coefficient of the linear term was even.

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}x + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

Now, here's the trick. We *add* and *then subtract* $\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}{4}}$ last:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}x  + \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2 - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2 + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

As a result, the first three terms now form a perfect square!

$$
\underbrace{x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}x  + \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2} - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2 + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

Factoring the perfect square, we get

$$
\left(x + {\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2  - \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac{3}{2}}}\right)^2 + {\color{purple}{4}}.
$$

Finally, evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+4 & = \\ {(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4}+4 & = \\ {(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4}+\frac{16}{4} & = \\ {(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+\frac{7}{4} & .\end{aligned}
$$

QUESTION 17592186272293
Complete the square on the quadratic expression ${x}^{2}+x-2.$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x-2
$$

Now, here's the trick. We *add* and *then subtract* ${(\frac{1}{2})}^{2},$ writing the constant term $-2$ last:

$$
{{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}}_{⏟}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-2
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-2
$$

Finally, evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
{(x+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4}.
$$


QUESTION 17592186272294
Complete the square on the quadratic expression ${y}^{2}+11y+25.$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{y}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{11}{2}y+25
$$

Now, here's the trick. We *add* and *then subtract* ${(\frac{11}{2})}^{2},$ writing the constant term $25$ last:

$$
{{y}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{11}{2}y+{(\frac{11}{2})}^{2}}_{⏟}-{(\frac{11}{2})}^{2}+25
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
{(y+\frac{11}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{11}{2})}^{2}+25
$$

Finally, evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
{(y+\frac{11}{2})}^{2}-\frac{21}{4}.
$$

LESSON Solving Quadratic Equations With Leading Coefficients by Completing the Square


EXAMPLE 17592186184083
Write the equation $2x^2 - 6x = 1$ in the form $(x+p)^2 = q,$ and hence solve the equation.

First, we divide out by the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}-6x & =1 \\ 2({x}^{2}-3x) & =1 \\ {x}^{2}-3x & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}\cdot x & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}^{2}-2\cdot (\frac{3}{2})\cdot x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4} & =\frac{2}{4} \\ {(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{11}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=-\dfrac32$ and $q=\dfrac{11}{4}.$ Now, we solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}\sqrt{{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{11}{4}} \\ |x-\frac{3}{2}| & =\frac{\sqrt{11}}{2} \\ x-\frac{3}{2} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{11}}{2} \\ x & =\frac{3}{2}\pm \frac{\sqrt{11}}{2} \\ x & =\frac{3\pm \sqrt{11}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186184084
Write the equation $-2x^2+2x+1 = 0$ in the form $2(x+p)^2=q,$ and hence solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-2{x}^{2}+2x+1) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 2{x}^{2}-2x-1 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}-2x-1 & =0 \\ 2{x}^{2}-2x & =1 \\ 2({x}^{2}-x) & =1 \\ 2[{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}] & =1 \\ 2[{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}] & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2[{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}] & =1 \\ 2{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{2} & =1 \\ 2{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{3}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-\dfrac12$ and $q=\dfrac32.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{3}{2} \\ {(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{3}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} \\ |x-\frac{1}{2}| & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x-\frac{1}{2} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x & =\frac{1}{2}\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186183914
Solve the equation $-2x^2 - 4x = 1.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that $x^2 + 2x + 1$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
x^2 + 2x + 1 = (x + 1)^2.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}-2{x}^{2}-4x & =1 \\ -2({x}^{2}+2x) & =1 \\ -2({x}^{2}+2\cdot 1x) & =1 \\ -2({x}^{2}+2\cdot 1x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}) & =1 \\ -2[(x+1)^{2}-{1}^{2}] & =1 \\ -2[(x+1)^{2}-1] & =1 \\ -2(x+1)^{2}+(-2)(-1) & =1 \\ -2(x+1)^{2}+2 & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by subtracting $2$ from both sides and then dividing by $(-2),$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}-2(x+1)^{2}+2 & =1 \\ -2(x+1)^{2}+2-2 & =1-2 \\ -2(x+1)^{2} & =-1 \\ (x+1)^{2} & =\frac{(-1)}{(-2)} \\ (x+1)^{2} & =\frac{1}{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+1)^{2} & =\frac{1}{2} \\ \sqrt{(x+1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}} \\ |x+1| & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \\ x+1 & =\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \\ x & =-1\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{aligned}
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186185352 Introduction
We can use the method of completing the square to solve quadratic equations with leading coefficients. 

As an example, let's solve the following quadratic equation by completing the square:

$$
4x^2-8x+3 = 0
$$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}-8x+3 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}-8x & =-3 \\ 4({x}^{2}-2x) & =-3 \\ 4({x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}) & =-3 \\ 4[(x-1)^{2}-1] & =-3\end{aligned}
$$

Now, expanding the brackets, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}4[(x-1)^{2}-1] & =-3 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}-4 & =-3.\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we isolate the perfect square by adding  $4$ to both sides and then dividing by $4\mathbin{:}$

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-1)^{2}-4 & =-3 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}-4+4 & =-3+4 \\ 4(x-1)^{2} & =1 \\ (x-1)^{2} & =\frac{1}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2} & =\frac{1}{4} \\ \sqrt{(x-1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}} \\ |x-1| & =\frac{1}{2} \\ x-1 & =\pm \frac{1}{2} \\ x & =1\pm \frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we simplify the solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}_{1} & =1-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2} \\ {x}_{2} & =1+\frac{1}{2}=\frac{3}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $x=\dfrac12$ and $x=\dfrac32.$


TUTORIAL 17592186185300 Deriving the Quadratic Formula
Consider the quadratic equation

$$
ax^2+bx+c = 0,
$$

where $a\neq 0.$ We can derive a general solution to this equation by completing the square.

First, let's subtract $c$ from both sides to isolate the variable terms:

$$
ax^2+bx = -c
$$

Since $a\neq 0,$ dividing by $a$ gives

$$
x^2 + \dfrac{b}{a}x = -\dfrac{c}{a}.
$$

Next, we complete the square on the left-hand side and move the constant terms to the right-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{b}{2a}x=-\frac{c}{a} \\ {(x+\frac{b}{2a})}^{2}-{(\frac{b}{2a})}^{2} & =-\frac{c}{a} \\ {(x+\frac{b}{2a})}^{2}-\frac{{b}^{2}}{4{a}^{2}} & =-\frac{c}{a} \\ {(x+\frac{b}{2a})}^{2} & =\frac{{b}^{2}}{4{a}^{2}}-\frac{c}{a}\end{aligned}
$$

Putting the terms on the right-hand side over a common factor of $4a^2,$ we get

$$
\left(x + \dfrac{b}{2a}\right)^2  = \dfrac{b^2 - 4ac}{4a^2}.
$$

Taking the square root of both sides yields

$$
x + \dfrac{b}{2a}  = \pm \sqrt{\dfrac{b^2 - 4ac}{4a^2}}
$$

which can be simplified as follows:

$$
\begin{aligned}x+\frac{b}{2a} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{{b}^{2}-4ac}}{2a}\end{aligned}
$$

Finally, we isolate $x{:}$

$$
\begin{aligned}x & =-\frac{b}{2a}\pm \frac{\sqrt{{b}^{2}-4ac}}{2a}\end{aligned}
$$

which simplifies as

$$
x = \dfrac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}.
$$

This formula is called the **quadratic formula**. You'll learn more about how to apply the quadratic formula in future lessons.


QUESTION 17592186184813
The equation $5{x}^{2}+2x-3=0$ can be written in the form $5(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q-p.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}+2x-3 & =0 \\ 5{x}^{2}+2x & =3 \\ 5{x}^{2}+2x & =3 \\ 5({x}^{2}+\frac{2}{5}x) & =3 \\ 5[{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{5}x+{(\frac{1}{5})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{5})}^{2}] & =3 \\ 5[{(x+\frac{1}{5})}^{2}-\frac{1}{25}] & =3\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{(x+\frac{1}{5})}^{2}-5\cdot \frac{1}{25} & =3 \\ 5{(x+\frac{1}{5})}^{2}-\frac{1}{5} & =3 \\ 5{(x+\frac{1}{5})}^{2} & =3+\frac{1}{5} \\ 5{(x+\frac{1}{5})}^{2} & =\frac{16}{5}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{1}{5}$ and $q=\frac{16}{5}.$ Finally,

$$
q-p=\frac{16}{5}-\frac{1}{5}=\frac{15}{5}=3.
$$


QUESTION 17592186184815
The equation $2{x}^{2}-4x-9=0$ can be written in the form $2(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}-4x-9 & =0 \\ 2{x}^{2}-4x & =9 \\ 2({x}^{2}-2x) & =9 \\ 2[{x}^{2}-2\cdot 1\cdot x+{(1)}^{2}-{(1)}^{2}] & =9 \\ 2[{(x-1)}^{2}-1] & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x-1)}^{2}-2\cdot 1 & =9 \\ 2{(x-1)}^{2}-2 & =9 \\ 2{(x-1)}^{2} & =9+2 \\ 2{(x-1)}^{2} & =11\end{aligned}
$$

So, $q=11.$

Next, we solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x-1)}^{2} & =11 \\ {(x-1)}^{2} & =\frac{11}{2} \\ \sqrt{{(x-1)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{11}{2}} \\ |x-1| & =\sqrt{\frac{11}{2}} \\ x-1 & =\pm \sqrt{\frac{11}{2}} \\ x & =1\pm \sqrt{\frac{11}{2}}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186247291
The equation $6{z}^{2}+10z-1=0$ can be written in the form $6(z+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}6{z}^{2}+10z-1 & =0 \\ 6{z}^{2}+10z & =1 \\ 6({z}^{2}+\frac{5}{3}z) & =1 \\ 6[{z}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{6}\cdot z+{(\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-{(\frac{5}{6})}^{2}] & =1 \\ 6[{(z+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-{(\frac{5}{6})}^{2}] & =1 \\ 6[{(z+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-\frac{25}{36}] & =1\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}6{(z+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-6\cdot \frac{25}{36} & =1 \\ 6{(z+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-\frac{25}{6} & =1 \\ 6{(z+\frac{5}{6})}^{2} & =\frac{31}{6}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{5}{6}$ and $q=\frac{31}{6}.$ Finally,

$$
p+q=\frac{5}{6}+\frac{31}{6}=\frac{36}{6}=6.
$$


QUESTION 17592186265780
The equation $4{z}^{2}+4z=12$ can be written in the form $(z+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, divide it by the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{z}^{2}+4z & =12 \\ 4({z}^{2}+z) & =12 \\ {z}^{2}+z & =3 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{2}\cdot z & =3 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot (\frac{1}{2})\cdot z+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =3 \\ {(z+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =3\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(z+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =3 \\ {(z+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4} & =\frac{12}{4} \\ {(z+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{13}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=\frac{1}{2},q=\frac{13}{4},$ and $p+q=\frac{15}{4}.$ 

Next, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}\sqrt{{(z+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{13}{4}} \\ |z+\frac{1}{2}| & =\frac{\sqrt{13}}{2} \\ z+\frac{1}{2} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{13}}{2} \\ z & =-\frac{1}{2}\pm \frac{\sqrt{13}}{2} \\ z & =\frac{-1\pm \sqrt{13}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272305
The equation $3{z}^{2}-15=12z$ can be written in the form $(z-p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, divide it by the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}3{z}^{2}-15 & =12z \\ 3{z}^{2}-12z & =15 \\ 3({z}^{2}-4z) & =15 \\ {z}^{2}-4z & =5 \\ {z}^{2}-2\cdot 2\cdot z & =5 \\ {z}^{2}-2\cdot 2\cdot z+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2} & =5 \\ {(z-2)}^{2}-{2}^{2} & =3\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(z-2)}^{2}-{2}^{2} & =5 \\ {(z-2)}^{2}-4 & =5 \\ {(z-2)}^{2} & =9\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=2$ and $q=9.$

Next, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}\sqrt{{(z-2)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{9} \\ |z-2| & =3 \\ z-2 & =\pm 3 \\ z & =2\pm 3 \\ z & =-1,\,5\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186317356
The equation $2{z}^{2}+4z=10$  can be written in the form $(z+p)^{2}+q=0.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we divide out by the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{z}^{2}+4z & =10 \\ 2({z}^{2}+2z) & =10 \\ {z}^{2}+2z & =5 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot z & =5 \\ {z}^{2}+2\cdot 1\cdot z+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =5 \\ {(z+1)}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =5\end{aligned}
$$

 

Then, we write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(z+1)}^{2}-{1}^{2} & =5 \\ {(z+1)}^{2}-1 & =5 \\ {(z+1)}^{2}-6 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

 

Hence, $p=1$ and $q=-6.$ 

Now, let's solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(z+1)^{2}-6 & =0 \\ (z+1)^{2} & =6 \\ \sqrt{(z+1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{6} \\ |z+1| & =\sqrt{6} \\ z+1 & =\pm \sqrt{6} \\ z & =-1\pm \sqrt{6}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186332962
The equation $3{x}^{2}-4x+1=0$ can be written in the form $3(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}-4x+1 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}-4x & =-1 \\ 3{x}^{2}-4x & =-1 \\ 3({x}^{2}-\frac{4}{3}x) & =-1 \\ 3[{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{2}{3}x+{(\frac{2}{3})}^{2}-{(\frac{2}{3})}^{2}] & =-1 \\ 3[{(x-\frac{2}{3})}^{2}-\frac{4}{9}] & =-1\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{(x-\frac{2}{3})}^{2}-3\cdot \frac{4}{9} & =-1 \\ 3{(x-\frac{2}{3})}^{2}-\frac{4}{3} & =-1 \\ 3{(x-\frac{2}{3})}^{2} & =-1+\frac{4}{3} \\ 3{(x-\frac{2}{3})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{3}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-\frac{2}{3}$ and $q=\frac{1}{3}.$


QUESTION 17592186332963
The equation $2{x}^{2}+10x+12=0$ can be written in the form $2(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side,  factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}+10x+12 & =0 \\ 2{x}^{2}+10x & =-12 \\ 2{x}^{2}+10x & =-12 \\ 2({x}^{2}+5x) & =-12 \\ 2[{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{5}{2}x+{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{5}{2})}^{2}] & =-12 \\ 2[{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}-\frac{25}{4}] & =-12\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{25}{4} & =-12 \\ 2{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}-\frac{25}{2} & =-12 \\ 2{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & =-12+\frac{25}{2} \\ 2{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{5}{2}.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{2} \\ {(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(x+\frac{5}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}} \\ |x+\frac{5}{2}| & =\frac{1}{2} \\ x+\frac{5}{2} & =\pm \frac{1}{2} \\ x & =-\frac{5}{2}\pm \frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}x & =-\frac{5}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{6}{3}=-3 \\ x & =-\frac{5}{2}+\frac{1}{2}=-\frac{4}{2}=-2\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $x=-3$ and $x=-2.$


QUESTION 17592186332964
The equation $4{x}^{2}-6x-3=0$ can be written in the form $4(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}-6x-3 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}-6x & =3 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}x] & =3 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{4}x+{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}] & =3 \\ 4[{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{16}] & =3\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-4\cdot \frac{9}{16} & =3 \\ 4{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4} & =3 \\ 4{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =3+\frac{9}{4} \\ 4{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{21}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-\frac{3}{4}.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{21}{4} \\ {(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{21}{16} \\ \sqrt{{(x-\frac{3}{4})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{21}{16}} \\ |x-\frac{3}{4}| & =\sqrt{\frac{21}{16}} \\ x-\frac{3}{4} & =\pm \sqrt{\frac{21}{16}} \\ x-\frac{3}{4} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{21}}{4} \\ x & =\frac{3}{4}\pm \frac{\sqrt{21}}{4} \\ x & =\frac{3\pm \sqrt{21}}{4}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186399760
The equation $4{x}^{2}+6x-5=0$ can be written in the form $4(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p+q.$

First, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+6x-5 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}+6x & =5 \\ 4({x}^{2}+\frac{3}{2}x) & =5 \\ 4[{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{4}\cdot x+{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}] & =5 \\ 4[{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}] & =5 \\ 4[{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{16}] & =5\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-4\cdot \frac{9}{16} & =5 \\ 4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4} & =5 \\ 4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{29}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{3}{4}$ and $q=\frac{29}{4}.$ Finally,

$$
p+q=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{29}{4}=\frac{32}{4}=8.
$$


QUESTION 17592186506525
The equation $3{z}^{2}-12=9z$ can be written in the form $(z+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $\,p+q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{z}^{2}-12 & =9z \\ 3{z}^{2}-9z-12 & =0 \\ 3({z}^{2}-3z-4) & =0 \\ {z}^{2}-3z-4 & =0 \\ {z}^{2}-2\cdot z\cdot \frac{3}{2} & =4 \\ {z}^{2}-2\cdot z\cdot \frac{3}{2}+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =-4 \\ [{z}^{2}-2\cdot z\cdot \frac{3}{2}+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}]-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =-4 \\ {(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =4 \\ {(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{25}{4} & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =4 \\ {(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{25}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

So, $p=-\frac{3}{2},q=\frac{25}{4}$ and $p+q=\frac{19}{4}.$

Now, we solve the equation using the square-root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{25}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(z-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{25}{4}} \\ |z-\frac{3}{2}| & =\frac{5}{2} \\ z-\frac{3}{2} & =\pm \frac{5}{2} \\ z & =\frac{3}{2}\pm \frac{5}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}z & =\frac{3}{2}+\frac{5}{2}=4 \\ z & =\frac{3}{2}-\frac{5}{2}=-1\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $z=4$ and $z=-1.$

$\,\,3(x\,\,+$
{{field-1}}$)^{2}=$
{{field-2}}.

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}+12x+8 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}+12x & =-8 \\ 3({x}^{2}+4x) & =-8 \\ 3[{x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{(2)}^{2}-{(2)}^{2}] & -8 \\ 3[{(x+2)}^{2}-4] & =-8\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{(x+2)}^{2}-3\cdot 4 & =-8 \\ 3{(x+2)}^{2}-12 & =-8 \\ 3{(x+2)}^{2} & =-8+12 \\ 3{(x+2)}^{2} & =4\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186184814
The equation $-5{x}^{2}+5x-1=0$ can be written in the form $5(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q.$

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-5{x}^{2}+5x-1) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 5{x}^{2}-5x+1 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}5{x}^{2}-5x+1 & =0 \\ 5{x}^{2}-5x & =-1 \\ 5({x}^{2}-x) & =-1 \\ 5[{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}] & =-1 \\ 5[{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}] & =-1\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-5\cdot \frac{1}{4} & =-1 \\ 5{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{5}{4} & =-1 \\ 5{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =-1+\frac{5}{4} \\ 5{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-\frac{1}{2}$ and $q=\frac{1}{4}.$
