QUESTION 17592186247295
The equation $-6{t}^{2}+5t-1=0$ can be written in the form $6(t-p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1:$

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-6{t}^{2}+5t-1) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 6{t}^{2}-5t+1 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}6{t}^{2}-5t+1 & =0 \\ 6{t}^{2}-5t & =-1 \\ 6({t}^{2}-\frac{5}{6}t) & =-1 \\ 6[{t}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{5}{12}t+{(\frac{5}{12})}^{2}-{(\frac{5}{12})}^{2}] & =-1 \\ 6[{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2}-\frac{25}{144}] & =-1\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}6{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2}-6\cdot \frac{25}{144} & =-1 \\ 6{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2}-\frac{25}{24} & =-1 \\ 6{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2} & =-1+\frac{25}{24} \\ 6{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{24}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{5}{12}.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}6{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{24} \\ {(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{144} \\ \sqrt{{(t-\frac{5}{12})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{144}} \\ |t-\frac{5}{12}| & =\frac{1}{12} \\ t-\frac{5}{12} & =\pm \frac{1}{12} \\ t & =\frac{5}{12}\pm \frac{1}{12}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}t & =\frac{5}{12}-\frac{1}{12}=\frac{4}{12}=\frac{1}{3} \\ t & =\frac{5}{12}+\frac{1}{12}=\frac{6}{12}=\frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

So, the solutions are $t=\frac{1}{3}$ and $t=\frac{1}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186247296
The equation $-4{x}^{2}-32x-61=0$ can be written in the form $4(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1:$

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{x}^{2}-32x-61) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{x}^{2}+32x+61 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+32x+61 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}+32x & =-61 \\ 4({x}^{2}+8x) & =-61 \\ 4[{x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2}] & =-61 \\ 4[{(x+4)}^{2}-16] & =-61\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x+4)}^{2}-4\cdot 16 & =-61 \\ 4{(x+4)}^{2}-64 & =-61 \\ 4{(x+4)}^{2} & =-61+64 \\ 4{(x+4)}^{2} & =3\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $q=3.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x+4)}^{2} & =3 \\ {(x+4)}^{2} & =\frac{3}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(x+4)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} \\ |x+4| & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{4}} \\ x+4 & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x & =-4\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186247299
The equation $-4{w}^{2}-16w+9=0$ can be written in the form $4(w+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1:$

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{w}^{2}-16w+9) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{w}^{2}+16w-9 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{w}^{2}+16w-9 & =0 \\ 4{w}^{2}+16w & =9 \\ 4({w}^{2}+4w) & =9 \\ 4[{w}^{2}+2\cdot 2\cdot w+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}] & =9 \\ 4[{(w+2)}^{2}-4] & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w+2)}^{2}-4\cdot 4 & =9 \\ 4{(w+2)}^{2}-16 & =9 \\ 4{(w+2)}^{2} & =9+16 \\ 4{(w+2)}^{2} & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=2.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w+2)}^{2} & =25 \\ {(w+2)}^{2} & =\frac{25}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(w+2)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{25}{4}} \\ |w+2| & =\frac{5}{2} \\ w+2 & =\pm \frac{5}{2} \\ w & =-2\pm \frac{5}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}w & =-2-\frac{5}{2}=-\frac{9}{2} \\ w & =-2+\frac{5}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

So, the solutions are $w=-\frac{9}{2}$ and $w=\frac{1}{2}.$

{{field-1}}$)^{2}=$
{{field-2}}, and the solutions are

$x=$
{{field-3}}
$\pm $
{{field-4}}.

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{x}^{2}+16x-11) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{x}^{2}-16x+11 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}-16x+11 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}-16x & =-11 \\ 4({x}^{2}-4x) & =-11 \\ 4[{x}^{2}-2\cdot 2x+{(2)}^{2}-{(2)}^{2}] & =-11 \\ 4[{(x-2)}^{2}-4] & =-11\end{aligned}

$$

QUESTION 17592186354469
The equation $-2{x}^{2}+12x-9=0$ can be written in the form $2(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q.$

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-2{x}^{2}+12x-9) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 2{x}^{2}-12x+9 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}-12x+9 & =0 \\ 2{x}^{2}-12x & =-9 \\ 2({x}^{2}-6x) & =-9 \\ 2[{x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}] & =-9 \\ 2[{(x-3)}^{2}-9] & =-9\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x-3)}^{2}-2\cdot 9 & =-9 \\ 2{(x-3)}^{2}-18 & =-9 \\ 2{(x-3)}^{2} & =-9+18 \\ 2{(x-3)}^{2} & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-3$ and $q=9.$


QUESTION 17592186399767
The equation $-4{z}^{2}-16z-7=0$ can be written in the form $4(z+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1:$

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{z}^{2}-16z-7) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{z}^{2}+16z+7 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{z}^{2}+16z+7 & =0 \\ 4{z}^{2}+16z & =-7 \\ 4({z}^{2}+4z) & =-7 \\ 4[{z}^{2}+2\cdot 2\cdot z+{(2)}^{2}-{(2)}^{2}] & =-7 \\ 4[{(z+2)}^{2}-4] & =-7\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(z+2)}^{2}-4\cdot 4 & =-7 \\ 4{(z+2)}^{2}-16 & =-7 \\ 4{(z+2)}^{2} & =-7+16 \\ 4{(z+2)}^{2} & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=2.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(z+2)}^{2} & =9 \\ {(z+2)}^{2} & =\frac{9}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(z+2)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}} \\ |z+2| & =\frac{3}{2} \\ z+2 & =\pm \frac{3}{2} \\ z & =-2\pm \frac{3}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$

QUESTION 17592186403072
The equation $-20{x}^{2}+60x-41=0$ can be written in the form $20(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-20{x}^{2}+60x-41) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 20{x}^{2}-60x+41 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}20{x}^{2}-60x+41 & =0 \\ 20{x}^{2}-60x & =-41 \\ 20({x}^{2}-3x) & =-41 \\ 20[{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}] & =-41 \\ 20[{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4}] & =-41\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}20{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-20\cdot \frac{9}{4} & =-41 \\ 20{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-45 & =-41 \\ 20{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =45-41 \\ 20{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =4\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $q=4.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}20{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =4 \\ {(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{4}{20} \\ {(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{1}{5} \\ \sqrt{{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{5}} \\ |x-\frac{3}{2}| & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{5}} \\ x-\frac{3}{2} & =\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}} \\ x-\frac{3}{2} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{5}}{5} \\ x & =\frac{3}{2}\pm \frac{\sqrt{5}}{5}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186403079
The equation $-3{x}^{2}-2x+6=0$ can be written in the form $3(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q-p.$

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-3{x}^{2}-2x+6) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 3{x}^{2}+2x-6 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}+2x-6 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}+2x & =6 \\ 3[{x}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}x] & =6 \\ 3[{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{3}x+{(\frac{1}{3})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{3})}^{2}] & =6 \\ 3[{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}-\frac{1}{9}] & =6\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}-3\cdot \frac{1}{9} & =6 \\ 3{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2}-\frac{1}{3} & =6 \\ 3{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2} & =6+\frac{1}{3} \\ 3{(x+\frac{1}{3})}^{2} & =\frac{19}{3}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{1}{3}$, $q=\frac{19}{3}.$ Finally,

$$
q-p=\frac{19}{3}-\frac{1}{3}=\frac{18}{3}=6.
$$


QUESTION 17592186404329
The equation $-4{w}^{2}-4w+11=0$ can be written in the form $4(w+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{w}^{2}-4w+11) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{w}^{2}+4w-11 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{w}^{2}+4w-11 & =0 \\ 4({w}^{2}+w) & =11 \\ 4[{w}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{2}w+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}] & =11 \\ 4[{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}] & =11\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-4\cdot \frac{1}{4} & =11 \\ 4{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-1 & =11 \\ 4{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =11+1 \\ 4{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =12\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $q=12.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =12 \\ {(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{12}{4} \\ {(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =3 \\ \sqrt{{(w+\frac{1}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{3} \\ |w+\frac{1}{2}| & =\sqrt{3} \\ w+\frac{1}{2} & =\pm \sqrt{3} \\ w & =-\frac{1}{2}\pm \sqrt{3}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186404336
The equation $-3{x}^{2}+36x-94=0$ can be written in the form $3(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q-p.$

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-3{x}^{2}+36x-94) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 3{x}^{2}-36x+94 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now, we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}3{x}^{2}-36x+94 & =0 \\ 3{x}^{2}-36x & =-94 \\ 3[{x}^{2}-12x] & =-94 \\ 3[{x}^{2}-2\cdot 6x+{6}^{2}-{6}^{2}] & =-94 \\ 3[{(x-6)}^{2}-36] & =-94\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we isolate the perfect square:

$$
\begin{aligned}3{(x-6)}^{2}-3\cdot 36 & =-94 \\ 3{(x-6)}^{2}-108 & =-94 \\ 3{(x-6)}^{2} & =-94+108 \\ 3{(x-6)}^{2} & =14\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=-6$, $q=14.$ Finally,

$$
q-p=14-(-6)=14+6=20.
$$


QUESTION 17592186404343
The equation $-4{w}^{2}+40w-75=0$ can be written in the form $4(w+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{w}^{2}+40w-75) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{w}^{2}-40w+75 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}4{w}^{2}-40w+75 & =0 \\ 4{w}^{2}-40w & =-75 \\ 4({w}^{2}-10w) & =-75 \\ 4[{w}^{2}-2\cdot 5w+{5}^{2}-{5}^{2}] & =-75 \\ 4[{(w-5)}^{2}-25] & =-75\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w-5)}^{2}-4\cdot 25 & =-75 \\ 4{(w-5)}^{2}-100 & =-75 \\ 4{(w-5)}^{2} & =-75+100 \\ 4{(w-5)}^{2} & =25\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $q=25.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(w-5)}^{2} & =25 \\ {(w-5)}^{2} & =\frac{25}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(w-5)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{25}{4}} \\ |w-5| & =\frac{5}{2} \\ w-5 & =\pm \frac{5}{2} \\ w & =5\pm \frac{5}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}w & =5+\frac{5}{2}=\frac{15}{2} \\ w & =5-\frac{5}{2}=\frac{5}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, the solutions are $w=\frac{5}{2}$ and $w=\frac{15}{2}.$


QUESTION 17592186506548
The equation $-4{x}^{2}-6x+10=0$ can be written in the form $4(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $p,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-4{x}^{2}-6x+10) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 4{x}^{2}+6x-10 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side and complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+6x-10 & =0 \\ 4{x}^{2}+6x & =10 \\ 4{x}^{2}+6x & =10 \\ 4({x}^{2}+\frac{3}{2}x) & =10 \\ 4[{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{4}x+{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{4})}^{2}] & =10 \\ 4[{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{16}] & =10\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-4\cdot \frac{9}{16} & =10 \\ 4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4} & =10 \\ 4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =10+\frac{9}{4} \\ 4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{49}{4}\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $p=\frac{3}{4}.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{49}{4} \\ {(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2} & =\frac{49}{16} \\ \sqrt{{(x+\frac{3}{4})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{49}{16}} \\ |x+\frac{3}{4}| & =\frac{7}{4} \\ x+\frac{3}{4} & =\pm \frac{7}{4} \\ x & =-\frac{3}{4}\pm \frac{7}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

We simplify the two solutions:

$$
\begin{aligned}x & =-\frac{3}{4}-\frac{7}{4}=-\frac{10}{4}=-\frac{5}{2} \\ x & =-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{7}{4}=1\end{aligned}
$$

So, the solutions are $x=-\frac{5}{2}$ and $x=1.$


QUESTION 17592186516838
The equation $-2{x}^{2}-16x-23=0$ can be written in the form $2(x+p)^{2}=q.$ Find $q,$ and solve the equation.

First, we multiply the equation by $-1:$

$$
\begin{aligned}(-1)\cdot (-2{x}^{2}-16x-23) & =(-1)\cdot 0 \\ 2{x}^{2}+16x+23 & =0\end{aligned}
$$

Now we move the constant term to the right-hand side, factor the coefficient of the quadratic term, and complete the square on the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{aligned}2{x}^{2}+16x+23 & =0 \\ 2{x}^{2}+16x & =-23 \\ 2({x}^{2}+8x) & =-23 \\ 2[{x}^{2}+2\cdot 4\cdot x+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2}] & =-23 \\ 2[{(x+4)}^{2}-16] & =-23\end{aligned}
$$

Next, we expand the brackets, and write the equation in the required form:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x+4)}^{2}-2\cdot 16 & =-23 \\ 2{(x+4)}^{2}-32 & =-23 \\ 2{(x+4)}^{2} & =-23+32 \\ 2{(x+4)}^{2} & =9\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, $q=9.$ We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}2{(x+4)}^{2} & =9 \\ {(x+4)}^{2} & =\frac{9}{2} \\ \sqrt{{(x+4)}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{9}{2}} \\ |x+4| & =\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}} \\ x+4 & =\pm \frac{3\sqrt{2}}{2} \\ x & =-4\pm \frac{3\sqrt{2}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

Solve the equation $-3x^{2} - 12x = 8$.

QUESTION 17592186272310
Solve the equation $5{y}^{2}-40y=-76.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${y}^{2}-8y+16$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{y}^{2}-8y+16=(y-4)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}5{y}^{2}-40y & =-76 \\ 5({y}^{2}-8y) & =-76 \\ 5({y}^{2}-2\cdot 4y) & =-76 \\ 5({y}^{2}-2\cdot 4y+{4}^{2}-{4}^{2}) & =-76 \\ 5[(y-4)^{2}-{4}^{2}] & =-76 \\ 5[(y-4)^{2}-16] & =-76 \\ 5(y-4)^{2}-80 & =-76\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by adding $80$ to both sides and then dividing by $5,$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}5(y-4)^{2}-80 & =-76 \\ 5(y-4)^{2}-80+80 & =-76+80 \\ 5(y-4)^{2} & =4 \\ (y-4)^{2} & =\frac{4}{5}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(y-4)^{2} & =\frac{4}{5} \\ \sqrt{(y-4)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{4}{5}} \\ |y-4| & =\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} \\ y-4 & =\pm \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} \\ y & =4\pm \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272311
Solve the equation $-6{y}^{2}+24y=21.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${y}^{2}-4y+4$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{y}^{2}-4y+4=(y-2)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}-6{y}^{2}+24y & =21 \\ -6({y}^{2}-4y) & =21 \\ -6({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y) & =21 \\ -6({y}^{2}-2\cdot 2y+{2}^{2}-{2}^{2}) & =21 \\ -6[(y-2)^{2}-{2}^{2}] & =21 \\ -6[(y-2)^{2}-4] & =21 \\ -6(y-2)^{2}+(-6)(-4) & =21 \\ -6(y-2)^{2}+24 & =21\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by subtracting $24$ from both sides and then dividing by $(-6),$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}-6(y-2)^{2}+24 & =21 \\ -6(y-2)^{2}+24-24 & =21-24 \\ -6(y-2)^{2} & =-3 \\ (y-2)^{2} & =\frac{(-3)}{(-6)} \\ (y-2)^{2} & =\frac{1}{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(y-2)^{2} & =\frac{1}{2} \\ \sqrt{(y-2)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}} \\ |y-2| & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \\ y-2 & =\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \\ y & =2\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272312
Solve the equation $-7{y}^{2}-42y=62.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${y}^{2}+6y+9$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{y}^{2}+6y+9=(y+3)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}-7{y}^{2}-42y & =62 \\ -7({y}^{2}+6y) & =62 \\ -7({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y) & =62 \\ -7({y}^{2}+2\cdot 3y+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}) & =62 \\ -7[(y+3)^{2}-{3}^{2}] & =62 \\ -7[(y+3)^{2}-9] & =62 \\ -7(y+3)^{2}+(-7)(-9) & =62 \\ -7(y+3)^{2}+63 & =62\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by subtracting $63$ from both sides and then dividing by $(-7),$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}-7(y+3)^{2}+63 & =62 \\ -7(y+3)^{2}+63-63 & =62-63 \\ -7(y+3)^{2} & =-1 \\ (y+3)^{2} & =\frac{(-1)}{(-7)} \\ (y+3)^{2} & =\frac{1}{7}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(y+3)^{2} & =\frac{1}{7} \\ \sqrt{(y+3)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{1}{7}} \\ |y+3| & =\frac{1}{\sqrt{7}} \\ y+3 & =\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{7}} \\ y & =-3\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{7}}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272313
Solve the equation $-4{x}^{2}+4x=-2.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}-x+\frac{1}{4}$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-x+\frac{1}{4}={(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}-4{x}^{2}+4x & =-2 \\ -4({x}^{2}-x) & =-2 \\ -4({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x) & =-2 \\ -4({x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{2}x+{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}) & =-2 \\ -4[{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{2})}^{2}] & =-2 \\ -4[{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}] & =-2 \\ -4{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+(-4)(-\frac{1}{4}) & =-2 \\ -4{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+1 & =-2\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by subtracting $1$ from both sides and then dividing by $(-4),$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}-4{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+1 & =-2 \\ -4{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}+1-1 & =-2-1 \\ -4{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =-3 \\ {(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{(-3)}{(-4)} \\ {(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{3}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2} & =\frac{3}{4} \\ \sqrt{{(x-\frac{1}{2})}^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} \\ |x-\frac{1}{2}| & =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x-\frac{1}{2} & =\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x & =\frac{1}{2}\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \\ x & =\frac{1\pm \sqrt{3}}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186388841
Solve the equation $4{x}^{2}+24x=-11.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}+6x+9$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}+6x+9=(x+3)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}+24x & =-11 \\ 4({x}^{2}+6x) & =-11 \\ 4({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x) & =-11 \\ 4({x}^{2}+2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}-{3}^{2}) & =-11 \\ 4[(x+3)^{2}-{3}^{2}] & =-11 \\ 4[(x+3)^{2}-9] & =-11 \\ 4(x+3)^{2}-36 & =-11\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by adding $36$ to both sides and then dividing by $4,$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x+3)^{2}-36 & =-11 \\ 4(x+3)^{2}-36+36 & =-11+36 \\ 4(x+3)^{2} & =25 \\ (x+3)^{2} & =\frac{25}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x+3)^{2} & =\frac{25}{4} \\ \sqrt{(x+3)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{25}{4}} \\ |x+3| & =\frac{5}{2} \\ x+3 & =\pm \frac{5}{2} \\ x & =-3\pm \frac{5}{2} \\ x & =-\frac{11}{2},\,-\frac{1}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186506516
Solve the equation $4{x}^{2}-8x=5.$ 

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}-2x+1$ is a perfect square.*

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-2x+1=(x-1)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}4{x}^{2}-8x & =5 \\ 4({x}^{2}-2x) & =5 \\ 4({x}^{2}-2\cdot 1x) & =5 \\ 4({x}^{2}-2\cdot 1x+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}) & =5 \\ 4[(x-1)^{2}-{1}^{2}] & =5 \\ 4[(x-1)^{2}-1] & =5 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}-4 & =5\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by adding $4$ to both sides and then dividing by $4,$ we get

$$
\begin{aligned}4(x-1)^{2}-4 & =5 \\ 4(x-1)^{2}-4+4 & =5+4 \\ 4(x-1)^{2} & =9 \\ (x-1)^{2} & =\frac{9}{4}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2} & =\frac{9}{4} \\ \sqrt{(x-1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}} \\ |x-1| & =\frac{3}{2} \\ x-1 & =\pm \frac{3}{2} \\ x & =1\pm \frac{3}{2} \\ x & =-\frac{1}{2},\,\frac{5}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186506589
Solve the equation $9{x}^{2}-18x=7.$  Write the solutions in ascending order.

*Hint: Use the fact that ${x}^{2}-2x+1$ is a perfect square.*

$\,{x}_{1}=$
{{field-1}} $,\,{x}_{2}=$
{{field-2}}

Using the hint, we have

$$
{x}^{2}-2x+1=(x-1)^{2}.
$$

First, we complete the square on the left-hand side:

$$
\begin{aligned}9{x}^{2}-18x & =7 \\ 9({x}^{2}-2x) & =7 \\ 9({x}^{2}-2\cdot 1x) & =7 \\ 9({x}^{2}-2\cdot 1y+{1}^{2}-{1}^{2}) & =7 \\ 9[(x-1)^{2}-{1}^{2}] & =7 \\ 9[(x-1)^{2}-1] & =7 \\ 9(x-1)^{2}-9 & =7\end{aligned}
$$

Isolating the perfect square by adding $9$ to both sides and then dividing by $9$, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}9(x-1)^{2}-9 & =7 \\ 9(x-1)^{2}-9+9 & =7+9 \\ 9(x-1)^{2} & =16 \\ (x-1)^{2} & =\frac{16}{9}.\end{aligned}
$$

We now solve the equation using the square root method:

$$
\begin{aligned}(x-1)^{2} & =\frac{16}{9} \\ \sqrt{(x-1)^{2}} & =\sqrt{\frac{16}{9}} \\ |x-1| & =\frac{4}{3} \\ x-1 & =\pm \frac{4}{3} \\ x & =1\pm \frac{4}{3} \\ x & =-\frac{1}{3},\frac{7}{3}\end{aligned}
$$


LESSON Completing the Square With Leading Coefficients


EXAMPLE 17592186184115
Rewrite $-x^2+6x+1$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $-1.$ So, we take out a factor of $-1$ from the entire expression:

$$
-(x^2 - 6x -1)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
x^2 - 6x - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 - {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{3}}x - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

Then, we add and then subtract ${\color{blue}{3}}^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}{1}}$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2 - {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{3}}x + {\color{blue}{3}}^2} - {\color{blue}{3}}^2 - {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(x-{\color{blue}{3}})^2 - {\color{blue}{3}}^2- {\color{purple}{1}}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(x-{\color{blue}{3}})^2 - 10
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}+6x+1 & =-((x-3)^{2}-10) \\  & =-(x-3)^{2}+10 \\  & =10-(x-3)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186184116
Rewrite $-2x^2+20x - 40$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $-2.$ So, we take out a factor of $-2$ from the entire expression:

$$
-2\left(x^2 - 10x + 20\right)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
x^2 - 10x  + {\color{purple}20}
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}2}$ as a factor: 

$$
x^2- {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue} 5}\cdot x + {\color{purple}20}
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${\color{blue} 5}^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}20}$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2- {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue} 5}\cdot x + {\color{blue} 5}^2} - {\color{blue} 5}^2 + {\color{purple}20}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
\left(x-5\right)^2 -{\color{blue} 5}^2 + {\color{purple}20}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
\left(x-5\right)^2 - 5.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-2({x}^{2}-10x+20) & =-2({(x-5)}^{2}-5) \\  & =-2{(x-5)}^{2}+(-2)\cdot (-5) \\  & =-2{(x-5)}^{2}+10\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186183943
Rewrite $-x^2 +7x -13$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $-1.$ So, we take out a factor of $-1$ from the entire expression:

$$
-(x^2 -7x +13)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
x^2 -7x+13
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 -{\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}x +{\color{purple}{13}}
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* $\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}{13}}$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2 -{\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}x + \left({\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2} -\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2+ {\color{purple}{13}}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
\left(x-  {\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2  -\left({\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2 + {\color{purple}{13}}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
\left(x-  {\color{blue}{\dfrac72}}\right)^2  +\dfrac{3}4.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-{x}^{2}+7x-13 & =-({(x-\frac{7}{2})}^{2}+\frac{3}{4}) \\  & =-\frac{3}{4}-{(x-\frac{7}{2})}^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$


EXAMPLE 17592186183944
Rewrite $2x^2 - 6x+1$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $2.$ So, we take out a factor of $2$ from the entire expression:

$$
2\left(x^2 - 3x + \dfrac{1}{2} \right)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
x^2 - 3x + {\color{purple} \dfrac{1}{2} }
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2-{\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}x +  {\color{purple}\dfrac{1}{2}}
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* $\left({\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}\right)^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple} \dfrac{1}{2} }$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2-{\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}x + \left({\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}\right)^2} - \left({\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}\right)^2+ {\color{purple}\dfrac{1}{2}}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
\left(x - \dfrac{3}{2} \right)^2 - \left({\color{blue}\dfrac{3}{2}}\right)^2+ {\color{purple}\dfrac{1}{2}}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
\left(x - \dfrac{3}{2} \right)^2 - \dfrac{7}{4}
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}2({x}^{2}-3x+\frac{1}{2}) & =2({(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{7}{4}) \\  & =2{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{7}{4} \\  & =2{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{7}{2}\end{aligned}
$$


TUTORIAL 17592186185337 Introduction
Let's think about how to complete the square on the following quadratic expression:

$$
- \left(x^2 + 6x+4\right)
$$

We begin by completing the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
x^2 + 6x + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with ${\color{red}{2}}$ as a factor:

$$
x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{3}}x  + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

Then, we add and then subtract ${\color{blue}{3}}^2,$ writing the constant term ${\color{purple}{4}}$ last:

$$
\underbrace{x^2 + {\color{red}{2}}\cdot {\color{blue}{3}}x + {\color{blue}{3}}^2} - {\color{blue}{3}}^2 + {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(x+{\color{blue}{3}})^2 - {\color{blue}{3}}^2+ {\color{purple}{4}}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(x+{\color{blue}{3}})^2 - 5
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-({x}^{2}+6x+4) & =-((x+3)^{2}-5) \\  & =-(x+3)^{2}+5 \\  & =5-(x+3)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186184448
Rewrite $-({k}^{2}-16k+8)$ by completing the square.

We begin by completing the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{k}^{2}-16k+8
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{k}^{2}-2\cdot 8k+8
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${8}^{2},$ writing the constant term $8$ last:

$$
{{k}^{2}-2\cdot 8k+{8}^{2}}_{⏟}-{8}^{2}+8
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(k-8)^{2}-{8}^{2}+8
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(k-8)^{2}-56.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-({k}^{2}-16k+8) & =-({(k-8)}^{2}-56) \\  & =-(k-8)^{2}+56 \\  & =56-(k-8)^{2}\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186184482
Rewrite $-x^{2} + 2x + 12$ by completing the square.




QUESTION 17592186272052
Rewrite $-({x}^{2}+4x-12)$ by completing the square.

We begin by completing the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{x}^{2}+4x-12
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x-12
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${2}^{2},$ writing the constant term $12$ last:

$$
{{x}^{2}+2\cdot 2x+{2}^{2}}_{⏟}-{2}^{2}-12
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(x+2)^{2}-{2}^{2}-12
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(x+2)^{2}-16.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-({x}^{2}+4x-12) & =-({(x+2)}^{2}-16) \\  & =-{(x+2)}^{2}+16 \\  & =16-(x+2)^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272054
Rewrite $-({y}^{2}+10y+20)$ by completing the square.

We begin by completing the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{y}^{2}+10y+20
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{y}^{2}+2\cdot 5y+20
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${5}^{2},$ writing the constant term $20$ last:

$$
{{y}^{2}+2\cdot 5y+{5}^{2}}_{⏟}-{5}^{2}+20
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(y+5)^{2}-{5}^{2}+20
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(y+5)^{2}-5.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-({y}^{2}+5y+20) & =-({(y+5)}^{2}-5) \\  & =-{(y+5)}^{2}+5 \\  & =5-(y+5)^{2}.\end{aligned}
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${(\frac{3}{2})}^{2},$ writing the constant term $2$ last:

$$
{{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}}_{⏟}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-2
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-2
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{17}{4}
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}-2({x}^{2}+3x-2) & =-2({(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{17}{4}) \\  & =-2{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+(-2)\cdot (-\frac{17}{4}) \\  & =-2{(x+\frac{3}{2})}^{2}+\frac{17}{2}\end{aligned}
$$

$$

$$

$$

$$

$$

$$
{{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{1}{4}x+{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}}_{⏟}-{(\frac{1}{4})}^{2}

$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
{(x+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-1.
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression::

$$
\begin{aligned}6({x}^{2}+\frac{5}{3}x-\frac{11}{36}) & =6({(x+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-1) \\  & =6{(x+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-6\cdot 1 \\  & =6{(x+\frac{5}{6})}^{2}-6\end{aligned}
$$


QUESTION 17592186272124
Rewrite $2{x}^{2}-12x+16$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $2.$ So, we take out a factor of $2$ from the entire expression:

$$
2({x}^{2}-6x+8)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{x}^{2}-6x+8
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+8
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${3}^{2},$ writing the constant term $8$ last:

$$
{{x}^{2}-2\cdot 3x+{3}^{2}}_{⏟}-{3}^{2}+8
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
(x-3)^{2}-{3}^{2}+8
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
(x-3)^{2}-1
$$

Finally, we substitute this result back into our original expression:

$$
\begin{aligned}2({x}^{2}-6x+8) & =2((x-3)^{2}-1) \\  & =2{(x-3)}^{2}-2\cdot 1 \\  & =2{(x-3)}^{2}-2\end{aligned}
$$

$$

QUESTION 17592186566232
Rewrite $10{x}^{2}+2x+\frac{7}{10}$ by completing the square.

$10{x}^{2}+2x+\frac{7}{10}=$
{{field-1}}
$(x\,+$
{{field-2}}
$)^{2}\,+$
{{field-3}}

Notice that the leading coefficient is $10.$ So, we take out a factor of $10$ from the entire expression:

$$
10({x}^{2}+\frac{2}{10}x+\frac{7}{100})
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{x}^{2}+\frac{2}{10}x+\frac{7}{100}
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{10}x+\frac{7}{100}
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${(\frac{1}{10})}^{2},$ writing the constant term $\frac{7}{100}$ last:

$$
{{x}^{2}+2\cdot \frac{1}{10}x+{(\frac{1}{10})}^{2}}_{⏟}-\,{(\frac{1}{10})}^{2}+\frac{7}{100}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
{(x+\frac{1}{10})}^{2}-{(\frac{1}{10})}^{2}+\frac{7}{100}
$$

Evaluating the last two terms, we get

$$
{(x+\frac{1}{10})}^{2}+\frac{3}{50}.
$$

$$

QUESTION 17592186566256
Rewrite $7{x}^{2}-21x$ by completing the square.

Notice that the leading coefficient is $7.$ So, we take out a factor of $7$ from the entire expression:

$$
7({x}^{2}-3x)
$$

Let's now complete the square for the expression inside the parentheses:

$$
{x}^{2}-3x
$$

First, we rewrite the coefficient of the linear term as a product with $2$ as a factor:

$$
{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x
$$

Then, we *add* and *then subtract* ${(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}:$ 

$$
{{x}^{2}-2\cdot \frac{3}{2}x+{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}}_{⏟}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}
$$

The first three terms form a perfect square, which we factor as follows:

$$
{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-{(\frac{3}{2})}^{2}
$$

Evaluating the last term, we get

$$
{(x-\frac{3}{2})}^{2}-\frac{9}{4}
$$
